The cancellation has one known gap, and it is the reason everything predeclared caps itself: the interpreter notices a cancellation between instructions, so a built-in halfway through building a huge list finishes building it first. The cap is therefore checked and the steps charged before the call does any work, and it covers the language's own sequence builders as well as our helpers — `list`, `tuple`, `sorted`, `set`, `dict`, `reversed`, `zip`, `enumerate`, `min`, `max`, `any` and `all` are predeclared again with the cap on them, because `list(range(1000000000))` is a billion values built inside one call that neither limit is watching. `match` is held to the same rule for the texts it reads: it folds each of them before comparing (5.2), which is work in proportion to the text inside one call, so each text costs a step a byte, charged first, and a text past the cap is refused — nothing that could stand on a card comes near it.

What a cap cannot reach is an operator. `[0] * 100000000` allocates through the language's own `*`, where there is no name to stand in front of, and starlark-go's guard on it stops at 2³⁰ elements, which is more memory than an instance has: a solver written that way takes the instance down rather than the request. There is no power operator in this language, which closes the other half of the same hole, and nothing else turns something small into something huge in one step — but a step repeated does it: `xs = xs + xs` twenty-four times is sixteen million elements in under three hundred steps, 480 MiB measured, and `s = s + s` does the same to a string. No step ceiling notices, whatever it is set to. Nor does the price of 6.5 reach a literal: `{}` is a dictionary of 512 bytes made in one instruction, so `[{} for i in range(n)]` keeps 512 bytes for every nine steps — 2.6 million dictionaries at the ceiling, and an instance of 1 GiB was killed for its memory by them (`docs/load.md`). `GOMEMLIMIT` closes none of this: it tells the collector when to work harder, and refuses nothing. The fix, if the gap ever bites, is a solver in a process of its own with a memory limit on it — a change of shape rather than a setting, and not one v1 pays for.

## 6.7 What the guide for the model says

The generation package (4.1) carries a page about the solver, and it is short on purpose. It states the contract of 6.2 with one worked example; it lists the helpers of 6.5 as a table; it names the six differences from Python that actually bite — no imports, no recursion, no `try`, `sorted()` not `.sort()`, `//` for integer division, and a `%` with one letter after it and a percent sign written `%%`; it gives the step and time limits as "roughly a million operations is fine, a billion is not"; and it ends with the one instruction that prevents most failures: **compute the answer, then return `match(options, value)` — do not write the letter yourself.**

The solver templates of `content/solvers/<topic>/` (R08, R64, О-43) carry the same shape per topic: one or two for every topic that has reference tasks, each the solver of one of them, generalised. A template opens with the kind of question it fits and the idea of its search, names the task it came from on a line of its own — `# From reference task ord-34-d2-2.` — and keeps that task's numbers and names in capitals at its top. The capitals are the places a model puts its own, and every word the solver matches against an option — a weekday, "It is impossible to tell" — is one of them, because the task is written in the chat's language and `match` compares text as it stands. Keeping the source task's numbers is what lets the bench run every template on that task and require its answer (6.8), so a template is a correct program to start from rather than a plausible one. The guide asks for the task first and the solver second, and for a search of the model's own whenever its task needs another. What the guide must not do is turn into a Starlark tutorial: a model that needs one is not going to write a correct brute force either.

## 6.8 Porting the prototype's 450 checks

The prototype proved every reference answer with a Python function that returned the answer as a value; a test then required that exactly one option matched it and that the option was the task's `correct_answer` (`tests/example_checks/`). v1 keeps the idea and changes the shape: **the check becomes the reference task's own solver**, written to the contract of 6.2 and stored beside the task as `content/examples/solvers/<id>.star` (1.5).

That is worth more than a translation. The 450 solvers then run through exactly the pipeline a submitted task runs through — the same dialect, the same helpers, the same limits, the same two runs — so they become the regression suite of the sandbox itself, and a change to any limit is tested against 450 real programs before it reaches a child.

**The bench** (T29a) loads every reference task, runs its solver twice as 6.2 requires, and fails if the verdict is not exactly the task's `correct_answer`. It reports the step count and the duration of each, which is what calibrates 6.6. Every reference task must carry a solver: one that has none fails the bench by name. The solver templates of 6.7 are run the same way, each on the reference task it names, and held to that task's answer.

**Each solver stands alone.** The prototype's files open with helpers shared by all fifty checks in them — `orders`, `unique`, `gaps`, `cuts_for`. A solver is one file with no `load`, so it carries the two or three it uses and no more. That is the same constraint the model writes under, and it is what keeps the bench measuring the real thing; the helpers are short, and a repeated one in fifty independent programs is not the duplication that costs anything, because nobody reads two of them at once.

**The step budget decides the shape, not only the size.** Python's own limits are patience and memory; this sandbox charges every value a helper builds against 6.6, and three ports of the prototype's checks ran out of budget written the way they stood. A search that rebuilds the same list for every candidate answer builds it once and reads the answers off it; a search over the subsets of twenty cards, or over the 32,768 ways six players could have played each other, is replaced by a search over what the question can actually tell apart — the cards grouped into couples, the games kept by how many each player has played. That grouping is not a shortcut past the enumeration: it is the same move the topic already makes when it counts a stock of balls by colour rather than ball by ball, and the answer it computes is still computed rather than assumed. The bench's report is how such a solver is found — the most expensive in the catalog spends a twelfth of the budget, the next one half as much, and nothing else comes near.

**What translates how:**

| Python in the prototype | Starlark in v1 | Where it appears |
|---|---|---|
| `itertools.permutations`, `combinations`, `combinations_with_replacement`, `product` | The helpers of the same name | Everywhere |
| `itertools.pairwise(xs)` | `for i in range(len(xs) - 1)` | `counting.gaps` |
| A string standing in for a sequence, `product("HT", repeat=3)` | Its characters written out, `product(["H", "T"], repeat=3)` | `combinatorics.enumeration`, `parity.alternation` |
| `itertools.count()` | `while` with an explicit counter | `time.clocks`, `pigeonhole.basic` |
| `f"{n:02d}"`, and any other width or precision | `%` here takes no flags: pad by hand, `str(n) if n >= 10 else "0" + str(n)` | `time.clocks`, `time.calendar` |
| `set(text)`, iterating a string | `set(text.elems())` — this language does not iterate a string | `time.clocks` |
| `math.prod` | `prod` | `arithmetic.tricks` |
| `collections.deque` with `popleft` | A list plus a head index: `head = 0`, `while head < len(queue)` | `algorithms.weighing_pouring`, `parity.alternation` |
| `functools.lru_cache` on a recursive function | Bottom-up dynamic programming over a `dict` — recursion is off (6.4) | `algorithms.weighing_pouring` |
| `fractions.Fraction` | Exact integers: multiply through by the denominators, or compare `a/b` with `c/d` as `a*d` against `c*b` | `arithmetic.tricks`, `algorithms.weighing_pouring` |
| `datetime.date`, `timedelta`, `calendar.monthrange` | `add_days`, `days_between`, `days_in_month`, `weekday`, `is_leap` on `(y, m, d)` tuples | `time.calendar` |
| `xs.count(x)` on a list | `len([y for y in xs if y == x])` — a list here has no `count`; a string does, and a list does have `index` | `time.calendar`, `parity.alternation` |
| A dict changed inside `for key in the_dict` | Loop over the keys from somewhere else, `for key in range(…)` — changing a dict while walking it is an error here | `parity.alternation` |
| A variable called `load` | Any other name: `load` is a keyword of the language, and the parser refuses it before anything runs | `algorithms.weighing_pouring` |
| `random.Random(seed)` sampling many runs | Rewritten as the invariant it was demonstrating, or as an exhaustive search over the smaller equivalent state space | `parity.alternation`, three checks |
| `assert` | `fail("…")` | Wherever the prototype's shared `unique()` guarded that the clues pin down one answer. There is no `load`, so it is written out in the solver that needs it: collect the answers the clues allow into a set, and `fail` unless there is exactly one. `logic.ordering` above all |

The `random` row is the only one that is not mechanical, and it is the one worth being strict about. Those three checks ran twenty thousand random games to show that the parity of the result never changes; the parity is the mathematical content of the task, and a solver that computes it directly is both shorter and an actual proof. If a reference task turns out to have no such rewrite, the task is replaced rather than the rule bent — sampling is not brute force.

All three had one, and all three became the invariant rather than a search: the sum that summing two numbers never changes (`par-12-d5-3`), the parity that taking a difference never changes (`par-34-d4-3`), and the one piece every break of a chocolate bar adds (`par-34-d5-5`). The exhaustive search over every way of breaking the 4 by 6 bar was tried first and ran out of the step budget — the multisets of pieces are far more than the question needs — which is the paragraph above about the budget deciding the shape, met once more. Each solver states its invariant in a comment, and where the invariant alone must single out one option, it refuses when it does not.

**The batches**, 150 checks each, ordered so that the hardest constructs arrive last, when the helpers and the bench have been exercised:

| Batch | Task | Topics | Checks | First meets |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | T29a, T29b | The bench itself, `combinatorics.enumeration`, `logic.ordering`, `counting.gaps` | 150 | the four combinatorial helpers, `pairwise` |
| 2 | T30 | `arithmetic.tricks`, `pigeonhole.basic`, `time.clocks` | 150 | `prod`, `Fraction`, `count` |
| 3 | T31 | `parity.alternation`, `time.calendar`, `logic.knights_liars`, `algorithms.weighing_pouring` | 150 | `deque`, `lru_cache`, the calendar helpers, `random` |

The reference tasks written for grades 5–6 (1.6) carry their solvers from the start: T37–T39 write each task and its solver together, against the same contract.

## 6.9 Four ports, in full

**Enumeration** — `enum-12-d1-2`, "how many pairs can be chosen from three children". The Python was `len(list(combinations(["Ann", "Ben", "Kim"], 2)))`.

```python
def solve(options):
    pairs = combinations(["Ann", "Ben", "Kim"], 2)
    return match(options, len(pairs))
```

**Calendar** — `cal-34-d1-3`, "how many days in March, April and May together". The Python summed `calendar.monthrange(2023, m)[1]`.

```python
def solve(options):
    days = sum([days_in_month(2023, month) for month in [3, 4, 5]])
    return match(options, days)
```

**Pouring** — the breadth-first search over jug states, the prototype's `steps_to` with a `deque`. A list with a head index replaces the queue, and a dict replaces the visited set.

```python
CAPACITIES = [3, 5]
GOAL = 2

def next_states(state):
    following = []
    for i in range(len(CAPACITIES)):
        following.append(state[:i] + (CAPACITIES[i],) + state[i + 1:])
        following.append(state[:i] + (0,) + state[i + 1:])
        for j in range(len(CAPACITIES)):
            if i != j:
                amount = min(state[i], CAPACITIES[j] - state[j])
                poured = list(state)
                poured[i] = poured[i] - amount
                poured[j] = poured[j] + amount
                following.append(tuple(poured))
    return following

def solve(options):
    start = (0, 0)
    steps = {start: 0}
    queue = [start]
    head = 0
    while head < len(queue):
        state = queue[head]
        head = head + 1
        if GOAL in state:
            return match(options, steps[state])
        for following in next_states(state):
            if following not in steps:
                steps[following] = steps[state] + 1
                queue.append(following)
    return match(options, "It is impossible")
```

**Weighings** — `light_weighings`, the prototype's `@lru_cache` recursion: the fewest weighings that always find one lighter coin among twelve. Recursion is off, so the same recurrence is filled in from the bottom.

```python
def solve(options):
    best = {0: 0, 1: 0}
    for coins in range(2, 13):
        best[coins] = min([1 + max(best[k], best[coins - 2 * k]) for k in range(1, coins // 2 + 1)])
    return match(options, best[12])
```

Four ports, four shapes: a one-liner, a comprehension over a helper, an iterative search, and a dynamic program. Between them they cover what the remaining 446 need.

---

# 7. The MCP tools

The contract between the server, the chat's model and the widget. The flows these tools appear in are [03-flows](docs/architecture/03-flows.md); this section fixes their names, their arguments and the shape of what they return. The JSON schemas themselves are written in T23.

## 7.1 The tools

| Tool | What it does | Called by | Draws a card | `readOnly` | `idempotent` |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `get_profile` | The child's profile, or the fact that there is none yet, plus the rule's recommendation. The profile is shown in the progress's Profile section, not on a card of its own (R148, R162) | the model | **no** | yes | yes |
| `save_profile` | Creates the profile or changes the fields the parent owns; with the adult's agreement, puts a damaged file back to its latest earlier version that can be read (R214) | the model | **no** | no | yes |
| `edit_profile` | Changes the fields the form in the progress's Profile section shows — the pseudonym, the grade, the interests, the skills left out, the language of the lessons, and the country with its state for the United States — and never makes a profile (R148, R185) | the **widget** only — `ui.visibility: ["app"]` | **no** | no | yes |
| `get_progress` | The overall rating with its rank and how far through it, each topic met or within reach with its rating, its rank and how that rank stands to the overall one (R147), mastered topics, recent answers, the misconception map, the profile's fields the progress screen shows — `pseudonym`, `grade`, `interests`, `excluded_skills`, `ui_language`, `country` and `region`, under the names `save_profile` takes (R91, R185) — where the profile's file is (R147), and what moved since the last answer and over the week, the overall rating and each topic by its own answers (2.10, R165). The free-form `notes` stay out: the screen does not show them, and a free text written about the child has no reason to reach a card | the model | yes | yes | yes |
| `read_progress` | The same payload as `get_progress`, for the widget's own screen: the widget calls it from the line at the top of a task card (R97) | the **widget** only — `ui.visibility: ["app"]` | **no** | yes | yes |
| `next_task` | Hands out the task written ahead, on the card it draws at once, when one is kept and fits (3.7, R235); otherwise opens a request, or hands back the one open, and draws the card the task will come to, which waits for it (R152) | the model | yes | no | **no** — a request open is handed back as it was, but a call while its task is on the card without an answer records that task as skipped (R246) |
| `get_package` | The package to write the open request's task from (4.1), in words alone | the model | **no** | yes | yes |
| `prepare_task` | The package of the task to write now, in words alone: the one a card waits for, or else the next task, written ahead, for which it opens a request; or that there is nothing to write (R235) | the model | **no** | no | yes, within the window |
| `submit_task` | Takes the written task through the checks and hands it to the child: the card `next_task` drew turns into it (R152); a task written ahead is kept instead (R235) | the model | **no** | no | **no** — each call spends an attempt |
| `read_task` | How the task the card `next_task` drew waits for stands: being written, on the card, or not coming (R152); a question that would only hear what the card knows waits for news, up to 4 s (R259) | the **widget** only — `ui.visibility: ["app"]` | **no** | yes | yes |
| `submit_answer` | Records the child's answer once, updates the ratings, returns how the answer went, which the card that sent it turns into (R286) | the **widget**, or the model for an answer given in the chat | **no** | no | yes |
| `show_result` | How an answer given in the chat went, on a card of its own below the task's: the verdict, the trap behind a wrong option, the picture of the solution with its total, the solution, the topic and how the rating moved — what a task's card turns into once answered on it. It reads the answer the service recorded, and a task with no answer yet is refused (R278, R286) | the model, once it has recorded an answer given in the chat | yes | yes | yes |

The tools cover the five capabilities of PRODUCT 4.1: the profile is split into reading and writing so that reading can be annotated read-only and a host can treat it accordingly, and written a second way for the card's form alone, by a tool that takes five fields and never makes a profile, so that neither the notes nor starting over is within a card's reach (R148); the progress is read by two tools that return one payload — `get_progress` for the model, which draws a card, and `read_progress` for the widget, which draws none, so that a card which opens the progress inside itself never makes a host draw a second one under it (R97). A task is asked for by `next_task`, which draws the card it will come to as the ask is made; the package, which no card may receive, reaches the model through `get_package`, and the card asks how the task stands with `read_task` until it is on the card (R152). The next task is got ready by `prepare_task` and written ahead, and `next_task` puts it on the card it draws (R235, R252). How an answer went is drawn by `show_result`, which the model calls when the card asks for it in the chat: only a tool the model calls draws a new card, and the card of the task stays as it was answered (R278).

**Names are final**, and nothing else depends on them being exact: the host prefixes them with the connector's name (`MathTrail:get_profile` in Claude), so every instruction describes a tool by what it does and never by a literal name (R07).

Each tool carries a human `title`, under its own name and among its annotations, and every annotation said outright, none left to the default a host would read into its absence (R215): `readOnlyHint` and `idempotentHint` as the table has them; `destructiveHint`, true for the two tools that replace what the adult set, `save_profile` and `edit_profile`, and false for the rest, which read or only add to the record of the lessons; and `openWorldHint: false`, since nothing here reaches beyond the parent's own file. A tool with no title, or that does not say what it does to that file, is not served, and every description says what the tool changes in the file, or that it changes nothing. A description says what its tool does, what it takes and returns, and when to call it; it tells the model nothing of what to say, to whom or how, which a directory refuses: the rules of a lesson are the instructions' to say and, where one applies at a tool's moment, that tool's words' too (R261). In the handshake the server names itself `mathtrail`, titled MathTrail, with its build, a sentence of what it is, the site as its `websiteUrl` and the site's icon, `<site>/assets/favicon.svg`, as its one icon (R216). Each but `get_package` and `prepare_task` declares an `outputSchema`, so a host that validates structured results can; those two answer in words alone (7.3, R102, R152, R235). No description is longer than 2,048 characters, as much as a host may keep of one (remark 46, R103).

**No tool takes a `student_id`** (О-41). The profile is determined by the token (02-auth), the free edition has one child, and an argument the model has to invent is an argument the model gets wrong.

## 7.2 What goes in

| Tool | Arguments |
|---|---|
| `get_profile`, `get_progress`, `read_progress` | none |
| `save_profile` | `pseudonym`, `grade`, `interests`, `excluded_skills`, `notes`, `ui_language`, `country`, `region`, `lesson_topic`, `signin_country_off` — all optional; `pseudonym` and `grade` required when there is no profile yet. A field left out stays as it is; a list given replaces the one kept, and an empty list clears it; an empty `notes` clears the notes, and an empty `ui_language` makes the lessons follow the chat's language again. `ui_language` is the language of the lessons: the tasks, the cards and the model's words (R144). The skills are ids of the catalog (1.4), which the tool's description lists. A call that asks for what the profile already says writes nothing (R99). Caps and types are 04-profile, checked by the tool in its own words rather than in the input schema (remark 36). The grade sets where the child starts when the profile is created (2.1); changed later it is a label — the ratings, the start and the trial series stay as they are (О-56). `country` is where the family lives, by its ISO 3166-1 alpha-2 code, and `region` its state when that country is the United States, by its ISO 3166-2 code; both are read without regard to case and held to the list of countries, an empty one clears it, and a country changed to another leaves the old one's region behind. They are there to count families by country (12.4), so the model sets them only when the adult says them of their own accord and never asks (R185). `start_over`, a flag, asks for a new profile in place of one that cannot be read or is in the Drive bin, with the details a first profile needs: the old file is set aside, not deleted. A profile that can be read is never started over — the call says so and writes nothing (R120). `lesson_topic` is the topic the lessons are kept to, by its id, held to the catalog like the skills, and an empty one gives the choice back to the rule; the model sets it only when the adult asks (3.6, R193). `signin_country_off`, true, leaves the country the adult signs in from out of what is counted, and false counts it again; the model sets it only when the adult asks (12.4, R220). `restore`, a flag, puts a file that is no profile at all back to its latest earlier version that can be read, once the adult has agreed to lose what was saved after it: it comes alone, and beside any other argument the call is refused with `restore_alone` and does nothing; a profile that can be read is never put back over — the call says so and writes nothing — and a file no earlier version of which reads is told as `corrupted` (7.4, R214) |
| `edit_profile` | `pseudonym`, `grade`, `interests`, `excluded_skills`, `ui_language`, `country`, `region`, `lesson_topic`, `signin_country_off` — all optional, the ones the form changed and no other, or the topic of the lessons alone, chosen on the card of a task (8.3), read as `save_profile` reads them; an empty `ui_language` gives the lessons back to the chat's language. Its schema takes nothing else: `notes`, `start_over` and `restore` are refused before the tool runs (R148, R214) |
| `next_task` | `language` (BCP 47, required: 7.5), the chat's, which a language the profile names wins over; `topic`, `grade_level`, `difficulty`, `reason` — optional, what the adult asked for, and `reason` is required when any of the first three is present, at most 300 characters (section 3.4). The topics, by id with the levels each is taught at, are listed in the tool's description. The language is read as `ui_language` is — its canonical spelling, a tag that names its language — and the rules are checked by the tool in its own words, `invalid_arguments` (5.9), rather than in the input schema (remark 36). While a request is open and younger than the window (11.2), a call hands back that request and applies none of its arguments, and says so when they ask for anything the request does not have — a choice, a reason or another language (R100). While the lessons are kept to a topic, a `topic` other than it is refused and the same one asks for nothing, a reason given for it alone included (3.6). A request open for a task written ahead is not handed back: the child now waits for it (3.7) |
| `prepare_task` | `language`, as `next_task` takes it; `topic`, `grade_level`, `difficulty`, `reason` — optional, the model's own idea of where the next task should stand, held to the rules of `next_task`'s (3.4, 3.7). A request ahead open and younger than the window is handed back as it is, its arguments not applied |
| `show_result` | `task_id`, the task on the card whose answer is to be shown, as `submit_answer` takes it (R278) |
| `get_package`, `read_task` | `request_id`, the id of the request `next_task` or `prepare_task` opened: the one whose package the model writes the task from, or whose task the card waits for (R152). `read_task` also takes `refused`, optional: the tries turned down the card has heard of, which it sends once it has heard the task is being written (R259) |
| `submit_task` | `request_id`, `task`, `solver`, `self_check` — sections 4.3–4.5 and 6.2. The brief is the request's and is not handed back: `brief`, which chats begun under the earlier guide still send, stays in the schema as an optional argument whose description asks for it not to be sent, and is read past (R241). The two parts of the task are taken as whatever JSON arrives and read by the structure check, in its own words (5.2, remark 36); a part sent as a string that holds JSON is read for the JSON it holds, as a client sends a field whose schema names no type (R137). There is no `language`: the task is written in the language the request recorded, and the checks read it from there (R100) |
| `submit_answer` | `task_id`, `answer` — `A`–`E`, or `?` for "I don't know", which is recorded as a wrong answer with `confused` set and no trap (R93) — and `hint_used`, optional and false when left out (03-flows). The answer is read as an adult or a model types it — in either case, with the spaces around it dropped — and anything else is refused by the tool in its own words, `invalid_arguments` (5.9), rather than in the input schema (remark 36, R101) |

## 7.3 What comes out

Every result has three parts, and the rule of 03-flows governs all of them: **the model sees everything, and so does the widget the result draws**. The split below is about what each part is *for*, not about who can read it.

- **`content`** — text. This is the whole lesson when no widget is rendered (О-10): the task read out, the result explained, the progress described. It is written for a model to relay, in the task's own language where the text is for the child, and in English where it is for the model.
- **`structuredContent`** — the widget's payload, and the same data in machine form for the model. It never contains the answer, the trap texts or the solution before the child has answered (PRODUCT 4.4, criterion 11.3), and it never contains the generation package — nor do the words of a tool that draws a card, since a card receives the whole of its result: the package travels in the words of `get_package`, which draws none (R152). A host may show the model the payload in place of the words — Claude Code 2.1 does (T46) — so the facts the model acts on are in the payload of every tool that has one, and the words add how to say them; `get_package`, whose package is for the model alone, has no payload but a refusal's and says everything in words (R102, R152).
- **`_meta`** — `ui.resourceUri` on the three tools that draw a card, `get_progress`, `next_task` and `show_result` (R148, R152, R278), with the flat `ui/resourceUri` beside it for hosts older than the nested key, as the library's own helper writes both (R87), and `securitySchemes` declaring oauth2 with the scope `mcp` on every tool. `ui.visibility` is left unset on every tool but three, which means the default: both the model and the app may call. Those three, `read_progress`, `edit_profile` and `read_task`, carry `["app"]`: the model has `get_progress` for what the first reads and `save_profile` for what the second writes, and a tool it could also call would be a second way to do the same; it has no use for the third, since it hands the task in itself (R97, R148, R152). Setting `["model"]` on the eight tools the widget never calls — `get_profile`, `save_profile`, `get_progress`, `next_task`, `get_package`, `prepare_task`, `submit_task`, `show_result` — was considered and rejected; the widget calls `submit_answer` itself, and a list that left it out would break the card. The only benefit of the restriction is defence in depth against our own widget, and a host that mis-reads the list would break text mode, which is the product's fallback rather than a nicety.

Two fields appear in every `structuredContent`, the one of `get_package`'s refusal included:

- **`screen`** — which widget screen this payload is for (8.2). The widget never has to guess from the shape of the data.
- **`last_answer`** — the outcome of the last recorded answer, `{"task_id", "topic", "correct"}`, or null. One line, and it is the compensating control for a lost `ui/update-model-context` (03-flows): a model that missed the widget's message still learns from its next call that the child has answered, and what happened. `get_package` has no payload when it hands the package; `next_task`, called just before it, carries the line in its payload and its words.

No result carries a moment, an age or the version of the instructions, in its payload or its words: the model has no use for any of them, and a host's review reads them as logging metadata. The ids a later call passes back — of a request, of a task — and the links to the parent's own file stay (R217).

`submit_task` adds **`child`** — `{"pseudonym": …, "grade": …, "ui_language": …}` — to every result, accepted or refused: the task card's top line and badge read it, and so do the waiting screen a refusal draws and its `{grade}`; `ui_language` is the language the parent chose for the lessons, or null. Every result of `submit_task` that draws a task, or a wait for one, adds **`language`**, the lesson's language its words are in: the task's on a task card, and on a waiting card the request's it waits for (7.5, R144). It sits beside the task, never inside its text, and costs no call: the tool has already read the profile (R96).

`get_progress` and `read_progress` carry the skipped tasks (R98): each topic's `skipped` count, the entries of the recent answers that were skipped rather than answered, marked `skipped` and carrying no outcome, and **`skipped`**, how many tasks were left without an answer in all — every topic's count added up, one the catalog no longer has too, the one number the card and the words for the model both say, the words naming the same five latest entries the card lists (R147).

Their `topics` are those the child has met — answered or skipped a task of — and those within reach (3.2), in catalog order. `overall` carries **`share`**, how far through its rank the rating has come (2.4), and **`grades`**, the three levels in ladder order, each with the first and the last rank its grades are matched with — `{"grade_level": "3-4", "first_rank": 5, "last_rank": 7}` —, which the words for the model name too (R213); and each topic carries its `rank`, its `share` and **`compared`** — `ahead`, `even` or `behind`, its rank against the overall one — all null together with its `rating` while the trial series runs and before the topic's first answer (R147).

`overall`, and each topic with a rank, carry **`change`** — `{"last_task", "week"}`, how the standing moved since the last answer and over the week (2.10): each null where it cannot be told, or `{"rating", "rank", "share", "moved"}`, where it stood before as it was drawn and the word for the move, `rank_up`, `forward`, `same`, `back` or `rank_down`. A topic's earlier `rating` is left out — no card draws a topic's rating, and this one, measured by the topic's own answers from where the overall level stands now, is no rating the topic ever had — and a topic first answered in the while carries only `"moved": "new"`. `change` is absent where neither while can be told: during the trial series, and where the history keeps neither. The words for the model add a sentence for each while that can be told — the overall rating from where it stood to where it stands, with its rank when the rank changed, and the topics whose own answers moved them, by how — and name no topic nothing of its own moved, and nothing of the days with answers (R13). A card of an earlier release reads past the field (remark 38); this release's draws it (8.2, R171).

They carry the misconception map too, as **`mistakes`** — `[{"trap", "times"}]`: each trap the child fell for in at least `MATHTRAIL_TRAP_REPEATS` answers of the history window (11.2), with how many, the most frequent first and, of two as frequent, the one fallen for last first; empty when nothing repeats, and with no profile. Only a wrong answer to an option counts, and a trap the catalog no longer has is left out. A trap is its catalog id — the card names it in its own words, the model has the catalog's — and nothing of a task or an answer is in it. The map reads the window rather than the per-topic counts, so that a mistake left behind drops off it with the answers it was behind (R136).

After the trial series they carry the review of 2.11 as **`review`** — `{"strong", "develop", "early", "steps"}`:
- `strong` and `develop` are `[{"topic", "reasons", "trap", "moving"}]`: a topic by its catalog id; its reasons by codes, `mastered`, `high` and `rose` for a strong one, `low`, `failures`, `hints`, `trap` and `fell` for one to develop; the trap its answers keep falling for, left out unless that is a reason; and `moving`, true for a topic to develop whose last answer was right, and otherwise left out;
- `early` is the ids of the topics too early to judge;
- `steps` is `[{"kind", "topic", "trap", "base"}]`, each `trap`, `rhythm`, `practice`, `unaided` or `begin` — a topic to begin, with `base`, the strong topic it builds on —, the step for every topic with no `topic`.

Ids and codes alone: the card says them in its own words (8.2, R180), and the words for the model tell the review in English, each step with its advice, and say there is nothing to point out yet when it names nothing — without a card those words are the review. A host that shows the model the payload in place of the words loses the advice there, as it loses the descriptions of the mistakes, which the payload carries by id too. During the trial series `review` is absent; every list in it is `[]`, never null (R176).

They carry the site the topics' pages are on as **`site`** — `{"url", "languages"}`: its origin as a browser writes it, `MATHTRAIL_SITE_URL` (11.2), and the languages it is written in, those of its dictionaries — and each topic its **`slug`** and **`site_page`** from the catalog (1.2). The card builds a page's address from them itself (8.3); a progress with no `site`, from an earlier release, is drawn with no links. The words for the model name each step of the review with its topic's page — `Enumeration (https://mathtrail.app/en/topics/enumeration/#traps)` —: a trap's step at the part of the page that tells the topic's mistakes, `#traps`, a topic to begin at the top of its page, and any other at how to help at home, `#home`; the step for every topic, and a topic whose page is not published, name none, and the model is told to pass a page on only as the result names it. The page is in the language of the lessons the profile names when the site is written in it, a longer tag read down to its language — `pt-BR` as `pt` —, and in English otherwise. The card links in its own language by the same rule, so the two differ only where the card speaks the host's language rather than the lessons': the parent chose none, or one the card has no words for (R186).

`get_profile`, `get_progress` and `read_progress` carry **`location`** — `{"folder", "file", "link", "others"}`: where the adult finds the profile's file in their Drive, which is the export, and each other file that carries a profile too, `{"file", "link"}`, for the adult to delete (R120, R147). A store with nowhere a person could open, the one in memory, leaves it out. So does a search for the file that Drive cannot answer just now — it failed, or took too long: the progress and the profile are shown without it, and the words say where the file is could not be found, rather than the call failing. What is no failure of Drive's is told as everywhere: a profile gone since it was read is read again, and found gone, and access taken back or ending is the failure of 7.4 (R148).

`get_profile`, `save_profile`, `get_progress` and `read_progress` carry **`recommendation`** — `{"topic", "grade_level", "difficulty", "goal"}`, what the rule would set next (3.2), or null with no profile, and `"chosen": true` beside them when the topic is the one the lessons are kept to (3.6). Their `profile` names that topic as `lesson_topic`, null while the rule chooses. The rest of the brief — the plot, the traps, the rule's reasoning — reaches the model with `next_task` alone. The two tools of the profile carry the parent's notes, so that the model can read back what the parent wrote; no screen draws them, and the progress does not carry them (7.1, R99).

`get_profile` and `save_profile` draw no card (R148). Their payload stays as it was, for a host that still draws a card of an earlier chat, and the words of a profile created, changed or started over ask the model to say in a sentence what was saved. `edit_profile` draws none either: the card that sent the change reads its result — `screen`, `status`, `code`, `problems` as `{"field", "code", "rule"}`, **`changed`**, whether anything was written, `last_answer`, and `profile`, the details as they now stand, the parent's notes never among them; with no profile it is `status: stale`, `code: stale_profile`, on the first sign-in, and nothing is made. Its words are for the model: that the adult changed the profile with the form, and the details as they now stand, the notes left out. The card hands them to the model with `ui/update-model-context` once something changed.

`next_task` draws the card the task will come to (R152). A request opened is `screen: coming` — **`request_id`**, `child`, `language`, the request's, and `last_answer` —, and one handed back adds **`already_open`**. Its words are a short paragraph for the model: which request is open, in which language, that the task is the model's to write, that it gets the package with `get_package` and that request id at once and hands the task in with `submit_task`, that where cards are shown the child sees a card waiting for the task, that until it is accepted the adult hears only that a task is on its way, and the last recorded answer — and no package: the card receives the whole of the result. A request already open comes back saying so: it asks for the task if the model has written it, and sends a model that has not — a turn cut short, say — for the package, so that an impatient second ask does not become a second generation racing the first (03-flows). The description says the same, for a host that shows the model the payload in place of the words, and the words end on what to do in a chat whose tools have no `get_package`: have the adult start a new one. A refusal is `screen: waiting` with `status`, `code`, `problems`, `{"field", "code", "rule"}` for each argument, as `save_profile`'s — the code from the closed list of 5.9 —, and `child`, so that the card it draws knows whose it is; with no profile the screen is `first_run`. A task written ahead handed out is `screen: task`, with **`task`** as `submit_task` hands it below, `child`, `language`, the task's, and **`topic_choice`** as `read_task` hands it, so that the card shows it at once; its words read the task out and send the model on to `prepare_task`. A request ahead taken over while it is written comes back `already_open`, and its words ask for its task (R235).

`prepare_task` answers in words alone, as `get_package` does (R235): the request whose task to write now — the one a card waits for, or the next one, written ahead, and then that nothing is said of it —, the same words of the request's language, the last recorded answer, and the package. With nothing to write — a task kept already, or the day's tasks over — the words say so and carry no package. Arguments no request can be opened from are refused with a payload, `status: rejected`, `code: invalid_arguments` and `problems`, the one result with one.

`get_package` answers in words alone (R102, R152): the request, that it is handed in with `submit_task` and that request id, that every text the child reads is written in the request's language while the package's reference tasks, templates and guide are in English (5.11), and then the package (4.1). It has no payload and declares no output schema, because a host that shows the model the payload in place of the words would otherwise show it a request with nothing to write the task from. A request that is not the one open and awaited is the one exception, since its `status` is what marks it a refusal: `screen: waiting`, `status: stale`, `code: stale_request` and `last_answer`, and words that send the model to the request open, whose package it can have, or else to the task on the card, or to `next_task`.

`read_task` tells the card how its task stands (R152): `screen: coming` with **`refused`**, how many tries the checks have turned down, while it is being written; `screen: task` with `task`, as `submit_task` hands it below, once it is on the card, answered or not, and — once the trial series is over — **`topic_choice`**, `{"chosen", "recommended", "site"}`: the topic the lessons are kept to or null, up to three topics the steps of the review are for, in their order (2.11), and `site` as the progress carries it; the card offers the choice of the topic only with it (8.3, R193), and `submit_task` never carries it, since the model reads that result; and `screen: waiting` with `code: stale_request` when no task of the request is coming — the request out of attempts or time, replaced, or its task taken off the card —, or `first_run` with no profile. Each carries `child` and `last_answer`, and none carries a `status`: a card asking is answered, never refused, and its line counts an answer. Given `refused`, a question whose answer would be the task still being written, with as many tries turned down, waits for news, up to 4 s: a write of the account that lands on the instance wakes it and hands it the profile written, which it answers from without reading the file again, since a read straight after a write may lag behind it; a write that leaves the task as the card knows it keeps it waiting, and with no news by the end of the hold, it answers as the file stood when it was read. Without `refused`, or with an answer that is news to the card, it is answered at once (R259).

The words of an accepted `submit_task`, and of a task handed in again while the card shows it, say that the model adds nothing of its own about the task until the child answers or the adult asks: not its topic, not why it came, not how to solve it. A host may keep only the start of the instructions, so the rule travels with the task as well as in them. The same words say that where the card shows the task the child answers on it, and the card records the answer itself, so the model asks for no answer in the chat; and that without a card the model reads the task out for the adult to read to the child, and records the child's answer the adult gives in the chat. The descriptions of `submit_task` and `submit_answer` say that a card records the answer, and leave what to do about it to the words and the instructions (R261). Told only to record the answer, a model asked the parent to type the letter into the chat, a message spent on an answer the card already had (R190).

`submit_task` hands **`task`** once a task is accepted — `{"id", "topic", "language", "question", "picture", "drawing", "options", "hint"}`, what the child may see and nothing more — on the `task` screen, with the `attempt` it took. `picture` is the description the card draws (4.4), or null for a task without one. `drawing` is always empty: a card of an earlier release requires the member and would draw no task without it, and it leaves the payload 30 days after the release that brings pictures (remark 72, R269). It draws no card (R152): the card `next_task` drew turns into the task, and the payload keeps the shape a card is drawn from, for a host that still draws one from an earlier list of the tools and for a card of an earlier chat drawn again. The task's id is its request's, `tsk_` in place of `req_`, which is how `read_task` finds it. A task that failed its checks is `status: rejected` with `code` the first failure (5.1), **`reasons`** — each failed check once, `{"code", "messages"}`, in the order of 5.1 — `unchecked`, the `attempt` it spent and **`attempts_left`**, on the `waiting` screen; the last attempt's code is `attempts_exhausted`. A task for no request that is open is `status: stale`, `code: stale_request`: the card shows the task the child already has, when there is one — a task handed in twice, the answer to the first gone astray, must not turn the card the child is working on into a wait — and waits when there is none. A task written ahead and kept is no refusal: its payload is the task the child is working on, on the `task` screen, or, with none on the card, `screen: waiting` with `code: task_kept`, which a card reads as a card no task comes to; its words tell the model to say nothing about it and to ask for no other task (R235). Handed in again, it is told as kept. The `content` of an accepted task reads it out in its own language — the question, the options A to E, the hint marked as only for when it is asked for — around English for the model; a refusal's lists every reason. The picture is the card's alone: the question carries every fact the task needs (4.4).

`submit_answer` draws no card: the card that sent the answer turns into how it went, in the task's place, and asks the chat for nothing (8.2, R286). The model reads the same payload. An answer recorded, or told again, is on the `result` screen with all that `show_result` hands its card (below) — **`child`**, **`language`**, **`task`**, **`topic_choice`** and **`site`**, built by the same code — and **`result`** — `{"task_id", "topic", "choice", "correct", "correct_answer", "trap", "solution", "hint_used", "rating", "trial", "already_answered"}`: the child's choice, `A`–`E` or `?`; whether it was right; the letter of the right option; the trap behind a wrong letter as `{"id", "text", "repeated"}` — its catalog id, what the child is told about it, and whether the child has now fallen for it in at least `MATHTRAIL_TRAP_REPEATS` answers of the window, this one among them (R136) — or null for a right answer and for `?`, which took no trap; the solution; the rating in the topic as `{"before", "after"}`, or null while the trial series runs and `trial` stands in its place (below). The answer is recorded once (03-flows): the task stays on the card with the answer it was given until the next task is asked for (04-profile), and the same task answered again — from a second tab, after a reply lost on its way, by the model after the card — is told what was recorded, word for word, with `already_answered` true, and nothing is written (R101). Only an answer the service recorded is told again: a task the file says was answered, which the service never recorded, does not open, and is taken off the card like any other whose seal is lost (R126). An answer to a task that is not the one on the card is `status: stale`, `code: stale_task`, on the `task` screen and with no `result`: the card that sent it stays on its own task. The words say what is on the card now, and an answer to a task whose answer the window keeps is told that its answer is recorded; with no profile at all the refusal is on the first sign-in, as `submit_task`'s `stale_request` is. One that is no answer at all is `status: rejected`, `code: invalid_arguments`, with `problems`. A choice of the topic that cannot be worked out — a catalog with no topic to suggest — is left out, and the answer counts all the same. The `content` tells the model, where cards are shown and it recorded the answer from the chat, to call `show_result` with the task's id for the card of how the answer went — an answer pressed on the card has turned that card into the same, and an answer told again names the card that took it — and to add one short sentence at most beside that card, retelling neither the trap nor the solution; and how to explain the answer where no card shows it — brief praise of the step for a right one; for a wrong letter, the trap first, then the solution step by step; for `?`, the solution; and one short reminder at the end, in the model's own words, of a mistake that repeats; and, as the card's line to the model does too, wording that does not show whether the child is a boy or a girl — around the task's own texts, quoted, in the task's language, and says how the rating moved or how far the trial series has got.

`show_result` draws the card of how the answer to the task on the card went, for an answer given in the chat: an answer given on the card turns that card into the same (R278, R286). It reads the answer the service recorded, and opens the seal only for a task that has one, as `submit_answer` opens it to tell an answer again: a task with no answer is refused before its seal is read. Its payload is the `result` screen with **`child`**; **`language`**, the task's; **`task`** — `{"id", "topic", "language", "options", "slug", "site_page"}`, the options the verdict names by their texts and the topic's page on the site, `site_page` false where the site does not publish one; **`result`**, as `submit_answer` records it, with **`solution_picture`** and **`solution_total`** where the task has a picture of its solution (4.4, R276) and the card can draw it; **`topic_choice`**, as a task's card offers it once the trial series is over (3.6); and **`site`**, where the topic's page is. An answer is written after the reply that took it (R255), so a call that finds no answer reads the file again, twice at most, 700 ms apart, before it says so. A task with no answer yet is `status: rejected`, `code: not_answered`, and its words add that an answer the model has only just recorded with `submit_answer` may still be on its way into the file, to be asked for again in a moment then, and only then; a task no longer on the card is `status: stale`, `code: stale_task`; an answer whose seal no longer opens is `status: stale`, `code: told_no_more`: the answer counts, and the model says so in a sentence and offers another task; with no profile the result is the first sign-in. A refusal carries `child` and the language of the task on the card, and the card says why it shows nothing. Its `content` says what the card shows and asks the model to add one short sentence at most beside it, retelling neither the trap nor the solution (R286); where no card is shown, it carries the whole of the review in words.

**During the trial series** (2.2.1) the child has no rating yet, only a series under way. `get_progress` then carries **`trial`** — `{"answered": N, "of": 5}` — in place of the ratings: no overall rating, no rating in a topic and no rank or share, while the mastered topics, the recent answers and the misconception map are shown as usual. `submit_answer` to a trial answer carries `trial` with that answer counted, in place of the topic's rating before and after; the fifth answer carries 5 of 5 and closes the series, and every result after it shows ratings. Outside the series `trial` is null. The `content` of both says the same in words: how many of the five first tasks are done, and that the rating comes after them.

## 7.4 Errors, refusals and the words they use

A refusal is not a failure. The two are answered differently:

- **A refusal the model can act on** — a task that failed the checks, a limit reached, a request that is no longer open — is an ordinary result with `status` set to `rejected`, `limited` or `stale` and the codes of section 5.9. `isError` stays false: the model is meant to read it, fix something and try again, and a host that paints errors red teaches the child nothing useful.
- **A failure of ours** — Drive unavailable, the profile unreadable, the sealing key gone — is `isError: true` with one sentence.
- **A call held back by a pace** (section 10) is the one refusal told the way a failure is: one sentence — too many calls in a short time, wait a moment and make the same call again — marked as an error, with no payload. It is decided before any tool runs, and a payload of the shape a tool declares cannot be made without the tool. Its line is a refusal of status `limited` all the same, never a failure (R121).

A write made after its call answered (7.6) cannot fail the call, and is told to nobody: the line of the write says how it went, as a warning when it was lost or failed. A cause that lasts — a Drive that is full, access taken back — stops the next call that reaches the file the same way, and that call is told.

The wording rules are the same for both, and they are not stylistic: no internal error text, no stack, no Drive message passed through (CLAUDE.md, "Errors"); no answer letter and no option text in anything `submit_task` returns, because that result draws a card (5.1); and every message names what to do next — fix this field, ask again tomorrow, ask for a new task.

Both are made in one place. A tool hands back either an answer — a refusal is one, marked by its `status` — or an error, and the frame every call passes through turns an error into a failure: one sentence chosen by the error's kind from a single table, never the error's own text, which is whatever the code that made it had in hand. The protocol library would otherwise send that text as it is. An error nobody named is told in the general sentence, and so is a panic, a payload that does not fit the tool's own output schema, and anything else that goes wrong below the frame. A tool that needs a sentence of its own adds a cause and its sentence to the table (R83). `submit_answer` has one: a task whose seal can no longer be opened — the key that sealed it has been retired — cannot be checked, so the tool takes it off the card, writes that, and tells the model in a sentence of its own that no answer was recorded and a new task is owed, error `sealed` (12.2, 04-profile, R101). A task that had its answer before its seal was lost is told in a second sentence of the same kind: the answer counts, and only telling it again is gone. What the library refuses before any tool runs — arguments that do not fit the tool's input schema — reaches the model in the library's words, which describe the model's own input (remark 36).

`submit_task` has a sentence of its own too. A task whose solver found every slot of the sandbox taken for as long as a run may wait (6.6) was never checked, so the model is told exactly that — "MathTrail is checking too many tasks right now, so this task was not checked and no attempt was spent. Hand in the same task again with submit_task in a moment." — error `busy` (R122). It is a failure of ours rather than a refusal: nothing about the task was judged, the sandbox turns it away before the profile is read, and the same task handed in a moment later may pass.

What happens to the parent's Drive, or to the file in it, has a sentence for each case, each saying what to do next (R118–R120):

| Error | Case | What the model is told to do |
|---|---|---|
| `revoked` | The access to Drive was taken back | Ask the adult to connect MathTrail again and let it use Drive |
| `expired` | The Google token would end before a call to Drive could | Make the same call again; connect again if it keeps failing |
| `storage_full` | The Drive is full | Nothing was saved, an answer given included; ask the adult to free up space |
| `drive_unavailable` | Drive asked for a pause, or failed, past its two retries | Make the same call again in a minute |
| `corrupted` | The file cannot be read and was not put back: it is a profile that breaks a rule, which is never rolled back, or a damaged file no earlier version of which reads (R119, R214) | Restore a version from Drive's history, or start over with `start_over` |
| `damaged` | The file is no profile at all — not JSON, cut short, of no profile's shape, or too large for any — and nothing was done: no tool mends it on its own (R214) | Tell the adult; with their agreement, `save_profile` with `restore` alone puts it back to its latest earlier version that can be read, and what was saved after that is lost; or start over with `start_over`. Either way the damaged file is kept |
| `in_bin` | The file is in the Drive bin | Restore it in Drive, or start over with `start_over` |
| `behind` | Drive answered with a state older than one this instance wrote, twice, or with the damage of a file this instance has just put back (R214) | Make the same call again in a moment |
| `unsupported` | A newer build wrote the file more than 30 minutes ago, or at no moment it says: edited by hand, or left by a version since withdrawn (R128) | Restore a version from Drive's history, or start over with `start_over`, which sets the file aside |
| `newer` | A newer build wrote the file within the last 30 minutes, as a rollout does | Make the same call again in a few minutes; `start_over` is taken all the same |
| `conflict` | The profile was written elsewhere three times over while this call wrote it | Make the same call again |

A call that fails as `revoked` or `expired` also asks the host to sign the parent in again. Its result carries the challenge a request without a token is answered with, and `error="invalid_token"`, in `_meta["mcp/www_authenticate"]`, from which ChatGPT starts its sign-in; every other host is answered `401` with that challenge in `WWW-Authenticate`, from which Claude shows its Connect button (9.2). The development sign-in gives no challenge.

## 7.5 Which language, and who decides

Two languages travel through the system: the lesson's and the pages'. Confusing them is the easiest way to show a child the wrong thing.

| What | Where it comes from |
|---|---|
| The **lesson** — the task's wording, options, hint and solution, the words of its card, and what the model says | `ui_language` in the profile when the parent chose one, and otherwise the `language` argument of `next_task`, which the model fills from the conversation (R144). Stored with the request and the task (04-profile). A task card and a card waiting for a task speak the lesson's language their payload names as `language` — the task's or the request's (7.3) —, and a card drawn before that field existed speaks the parent's choice, as it did; a language the widget has no words for gives way to the host's (8.6). Before `next_task`'s result its card speaks the language the call was made with, its `language` argument, which the host tells the card with the call's arguments (8.4), and says nothing until the host has told it; the wait turns to the parent's choice as the result comes only when that differs from the chat's (R144, R190) |
| The **other cards** — the progress, the profile, the first sign-in | `ui_language` in the profile when the parent chose one, read from `profile` in the payload, and otherwise the host's `locale` from the app context (8.4), as R129 has it. A card drawn for `next_task` whose payload names no language — a refusal of the ask, the first sign-in — speaks the language the call was made with before the host's, as its wait did (R190) |
| **Server-rendered pages** — the consent screen, error pages | Nowhere: they are in English whatever the browser reads (8.9) |

**There is no fallback** (R100). `language` is required even when the profile names a language of its own, because a profile that names none would otherwise leave the lesson to a guess: a call without it is refused by the protocol library before the tool runs, as its input schema says, and a language that is empty or names none is refused by the tool, `invalid_arguments` (5.9). A language guessed from a host's locale would be a task written in a language the child may not read, a generation wasted, so the tool's description and the instructions both say the argument is always passed. When the profile names a language, it wins over the argument: the request is opened in it, and the model is told to write the task and to talk in it. The task is written in the language the request recorded; `submit_task` takes none of its own, and the checks read the request's (5.5, 5.6, 5.11).

## 7.6 Two writes at once

Drive has no conditional write, so what keeps two writes of one profile from laying one over the other is the service's own (05-storage, "Two tabs"; R116, R118):

- A write reads the file once more and compares it with the state it was computed from. One that finds it changed writes nothing, and is refused as a conflict. The day's first write asks Drive to keep its revision, and deletes nothing from the file's history to make room for it (R214).
- Within an instance the writes of one account take turns, so two tabs served by one instance never meet between the read and the upload.
- A tool whose write was refused, or found the profile deleted before it, reads again and decides again, up to three times. What the other writer did is found done — the request already open, the answer already recorded, the task already accepted — and nothing is sent twice.

**The window that remains.** Two instances share nothing. When the calls of two tabs land on two instances, and each reads the file again before the other's upload lands, both uploads land: Drive keeps the later one whole, and the earlier is gone from the head of the file, though still in its history. The window is as wide as one upload, a few hundred milliseconds, and it takes two people acting within it on two instances. What it can cost is one change, and each makes itself again: an answer lost leaves the task on the card, and the child's next press records it; a request lost makes the task handed in for it `stale_request`, and the model asks again; an edit lost is one the adult makes again. The daily counters can undercount by one (10.4). Nothing in Drive's API lets the service see that it happened: asking which revision came before its own would cost a call on every write. The store's tests hold both sides of it against the stand-in Drive — on one instance, of eight saves made from one revision exactly one lands; on two, two overlapping uploads both land, and the later is what the file holds.

**Writes after the answer** (R255). Two writes the child need not wait for are made once their call has answered: `next_task` handing out the task written ahead, and `submit_answer` recording an answer. The request is held open until the write is done, at most 31 s, so the instance keeps its CPU for it. A write that finds the file changed, or Drive out of reach, is made again on a fresh read by the tool's own change while the change still applies, up to three tries in 30 s, and its lines are written once the file holds it; `write_after_answer` says how it went (12.2). An upload Drive failed on may have landed all the same, and a fresh read that finds the change at the number the write gave the profile counts it as written. A hand-out that did not land leaves the card with a task the file does not have on it: an answer given there is refused as stale, and the next ask hands the task out again while the file keeps it. On one instance a call of the same account waits for the writes begun before it, so the model's `prepare_task` after `next_task` finds the task handed out. On two, a call within about 1.7 s of the answer reads the file as it was: `prepare_task` there writes nothing ahead that turn, and an answer may be lost, to a skip or to another answer to the same task, which its line says as a warning. A stop of the instance waits for such a write as for any request in flight, at most the drain's 6.75 s (9.2); one still under way then is cut short and its change lost, with no line of its own, and the line of the stop says the drain was cut short. Any write of an account that lands on an instance, after the answer or before it, is also news to the card's questions held there for that account, and hands them the profile written (R259).

---

# 8. Widgets, screens and languages

Everything here is the MCP Apps extension 2026-01-26 as the `@modelcontextprotocol/ext-apps` library implements it — 2.0.3, the version `web/package.json` pins, raised from 2.0.0 by a patch release (R06); where the library and the specification text disagree, the library wins (R06). What T03 confirmed live in Claude is marked as such.

## 8.1 One resource

| Property | Value |
|---|---|
| URI | `ui://mathtrail/app.html` |
| MIME type | `text/html;profile=mcp-app` |
| Contents | One HTML file with the Preact bundle, the styles and every dictionary inlined — no external load of any kind (О-13, PRODUCT 4.2) |
| `_meta.ui.csp` | All four lists empty: `connectDomains`, `resourceDomains`, `frameDomains`, `baseUriDomains`. Empty is the secure default in the library — no network, no third-party resources, no nested frames |
| `_meta.ui.permissions` | None requested: no camera, no microphone, nothing |
| `_meta.ui.domain` | Not set: Claude makes the sandbox's origin itself and draws no card for a page that names another (R216) |
| `_meta["openai/widgetDomain"]` | The service's public origin, `MATHTRAIL_PUBLIC_URL`, which ChatGPT makes the page's sandbox from: an origin of the plugin's own, as its review asks (R216) |
| `_meta["openai/widgetCSP"]` | ChatGPT's own form of the policy: `connect_domains`, `resource_domains` and `frame_domains` empty, as above, and `redirect_domains` the site's origin alone, `MATHTRAIL_SITE_URL` — the one place a card's links lead, which ChatGPT then opens without a warning first (8.3, R216) |
| `_meta.ui.prefersBorder` | `false` — the card draws its own boundary, and a host's frame around it would be a second card around the first. ChatGPT reads the same key, and its own, `openai/widgetPrefersBorder`, is only an alias of it, so the key is not sent twice. Said rather than left out: a host left to choose decides for itself, and Claude then frames the card on a phone alone (R265) |
| Where `_meta` travels | Both with the resource where it is listed and with the page where it is read: a host takes what came with the page and falls back to the listing (R87) |

The empty CSP is worth stating as a property rather than a setting: a widget that cannot reach the network cannot leak what it holds, and what it holds is a child's task. A page of the site the progress links to is no load of the card's: the host opens it, outside the card, and its address is the site's origin, a language and a topic's slug, with nothing of the child in it (8.3, R186).

## 8.2 The seven screens

One resource, seven screens, and the payload says which — `structuredContent.screen` (7.3). The widget never infers a screen from the shape of the data, because a wrong guess would show a child the wrong thing.

| `screen` | Drawn by | Shows |
|---|---|---|
| `first_run` | `get_progress`, `read_progress` and `submit_task` finding no profile (7.3); a card of an earlier chat from `get_profile` or `save_profile` | What the app is, the rule of the pseudonym, what the chat will ask, where it is kept and how to start: by asking in the chat, where the adult says they are the child's parent or tutor and the model makes the profile. It only tells, with nothing to tick and nothing to press (R148). A first profile refused, on a card of an earlier chat, says it was not made |
| `profile` | A card of an earlier chat, drawn from `get_profile` or `save_profile`, which draw none now (R148) | Pseudonym, grade, interests, constraints, the language of the lessons and the country with its state — never the notes — with "Edit" at their head, which opens the form the progress has. Under them, where the payload says where the file is, the parent's data: where the file is, which is the export, and the other files that hold a profile; how to delete it, cut the service off and remove the app — named, never opened (R135). A change refused says nothing was saved, until one is saved on the card |
| `progress` | `get_progress`, and the widget itself from the line at the top of a card with `read_progress` (R91, R97) | After the author's mockups (R147, R162, R171): once the trial series is over and the progress tells moves (2.10), a switch of two segments, "Last task" and "Past week", choosing the while they are drawn over — the week while it can be told, else the last task, kept as the sections are —; the overall rank, large, by its number — "Rank 3" — beside "of 11" — the rating's number is the model's to say —, a course of the eleven ranks filled as far as the rating has come, striped on from or back to where it stood over the while chosen in the green of a gain or the coral of a step back — a move the words call one striped over a seventh of a step at least —; under the course, for the parent, the grades whose tasks each run of ranks roughly matches — a bracket under each run's steps with its words, the run reached drawn stronger — and a note that this is a rough guide to olympiad tasks and no school mark (2.4, R213); a line of how it moved, or that there is nothing to compare the while with yet, and the next rank to reach — during the trial series, how many of its five tasks are done instead (7.3) —; and what comes next, with a line under it when it is the topic chosen for the lessons (3.6). Under them, sections that fold away under their titles, the topics open and the rest folded as the screen first opens, and open as they were left, for as long as the card is drawn, when the progress over it is opened again; a section with nothing in it is not drawn. Topics: each topic met or within reach with its rank, by its number, the topic's name a link to its page on the site where the host opens links (8.3); the word of its own move over the while chosen where it moved — a new rank, forward, back, the rank below, a new topic — and otherwise "ahead", "even" or "behind" as its rank stands to the overall one; the mark of a mastered one; and its own course in a ramp of five colours from orange to green by its rank, striped where it moved, drawn open with "no answers yet" while it has no answer and "no rank yet" during the trial series. By the section's title, how many topics moved up — a new one among them — and how many back, and at its head a legend of the stripes and the while. Once the trial series is over, the review of the topics (2.11), with "strengths and plan" by its title, in parts under headings in their colours (R180): the strong topics and the ones to develop, each after a dot in the green or the coral of its side, with a sentence for each reason it is named for and, for one to develop whose last answer was right, that a move has begun; the topics too early to judge, on a line with why there is no verdict; the mistakes that repeat — each in plain words with how many times and a dot for each, up to five (R136) —; and what to do next, numbered, each step the topic it is for — a link to the part of its page the step is about — over its advice, a topic to begin last, over a sentence of the strong topic it builds on, and the step for every topic with its advice alone. What the card has no words for — a reason, a kind of step, a mistake's advice, a mistake's name inside a sentence, or the name of the base of a topic to begin, that a later release adds — it leaves out, and so it does a step to begin that names no topic; a topic named for nothing it can say keeps its name, and a mistake that repeats is listed by its id, as every name the card lacks is. While the trial series runs, the mistakes that repeat are a section of their own, before the latest answers. The latest answers — a dot for each of the five shown, in the colour of how it went, by the section's title, and the same in words for a screen reader — with the skipped tasks among them and how many were left without an answer in all (R98). The profile, "for the parent": the fields 7.1 names, with "Edit" at their head, which opens a form in their place: the pseudonym, the grade, the interests as chips added and taken out, the skills left out as ticks and the language of the lessons, never the notes, saved by `edit_profile` (R148); and the parent's data, under the questions it answers, where the progress says where the file is (R120) — a search that failed shows none (R148). The ranks are told by their numbers, with no names (R213). |
| `coming` | `next_task` opening a request or handing back the one open (R152) | At the top, the child's pseudonym and "Profile & progress", and the grade as a badge, as on the task card. "Preparing the next task…" with the topic picked and the task being written — the checks and the task ready not claimed while the task is awaited, since the card cannot see them — and, under them, a try the checks turned down, with a new one being written, and, two minutes after the wait last had news, that the task is taking longer than usual and can be asked for in the chat if it does not appear. The card asks `read_task` how the task stands (8.3); once the task is on the card, a card that waited for it ticks off the checks and the task ready at once, both true by then, since a task reaches the card only after every check has passed, and 300 ms later turns into the task, in the same card (R259); a card whose first answer brings the task, one drawn again with an earlier chat, turns into it at once (R201); a request that is over — whatever ended it, since the card cannot tell — says no task is here, with the line that the next one comes below. Nothing on it asks for anything |
| `task` | `read_task` once the task is accepted, in the card `next_task` drew; `next_task` handing out the task written ahead, in the card it draws (R235); `submit_task`'s own payload, for a host that still draws a card for it from an earlier list of the tools and for a card of an earlier chat (R152) | At the top, the child's pseudonym and "Profile & progress", and the grade as a badge, both from `child` (R96); wording, the picture drawn from its description (4.4, 8.12.1), the five options as buttons with their letters and texts (R90), under a legend a screen reader alone hears (R263); Hint and Another task, each a button of its icon alone — a bulb on amber, two arrows in a circle on blue — named by its words to a screen reader and under a pointer (R263), and no "I don't know", which the adult says in the chat (R208, R261) — once another task is asked for, the card keeps its task and says that, once the ask reaches the chat, the new one will come below, in a card of its own (R145), and once the chat has taken the ask the card is done with: its options locked, its buttons and its choice of the topic gone (R252). Once answered, the card turns into how the answer went, in the task's place and under the same header — what the `result` row below draws, from `submit_answer`'s own payload — and asks the chat for nothing; an answer recorded before is shown as it was recorded, under "This task already has its answer" (8.3, R286). Once the trial series is over, `read_task`'s task carries the choice of the topic (7.3), and the row ends with its button, in green — a tag, "Topic:" and the topic the lessons are kept to, or a spark and that the coach chooses; on a narrow card the topic alone, or "Coach", with "Topic:" left to a screen reader (R263) —, which opens the choice under the row (8.3); a task on the topic chosen, as `read_task` names it, has a mark of it above its wording while the choice stands, with a cross that gives the choice back to the coach — a task whose own topic is chosen on its card gets none (3.6, R194). A card with no choice in its payload — in the trial series, of an earlier release, or on the site's page "Why" — has neither; the home page's cards carry the coach's choice and show the button locked (8.12) |
| `waiting` | `next_task` refusing a request — the day's limit, or arguments no request can be opened from —; and `submit_task` refusing a try or a request that is not open, for a host and a chat of before R152 | At the top, the child's pseudonym and "Profile & progress", as on the task card. What happened and what comes next, with nothing that moves or asks (R146): a try that did not pass the checks, a request that is over, or the last attempt refused (R145), each with the line that the next one comes below and, if none does, that a task can be asked for in the chat — the card cannot see the next try being written, which comes in a card of its own. A refusal for the day, `status: limited`, shows the words of 10.3 and nothing to press: the card `next_task` draws for the ask receives it (remark 61, R134, R152). Arguments refused say no task is here |
| `result` | `show_result`, on a card of its own below the task's, for an answer given in the chat (R278); the same under a task's own header once it is answered on its card (R286) | At the top, the child's pseudonym and "Profile & progress", and the grade as a badge, as on the task card. Right or wrong, with the right option and the one chosen by their texts; the trap behind the chosen option, with "This mistake has come up before" under it when it repeats (R136); the picture of the solution with its total under it, and after a wrong option the option it was not (4.4, R276); the solution step by step (R132); then a band of the topic — "Type of task" and its name, a link to its page where the host opens links (8.3) — and the rating in the topic before and after, the after in the colour of the move — during the trial series, N of 5 instead (7.3). Under it, Another task and, once the trial series is over, the choice of the topic, which ask the chat as a task's card does, after which the card is done with (R252). After "I don't know", said in the chat and told to an option pressed after it as the answer recorded first (R208), the solution with no right-or-wrong line — but the rating before and after, since it counts as a wrong answer (R93).
