A task with no answer yet, or no longer on the card, says so and shows nothing of it, and an answer whose seal no longer opens says it is recorded and counts |

**Before its result**, a card the host drew for `next_task` — as `toolInfo` names it (8.4) — shows the wait for the task asked for, which no payload draws (R152): after a moment, so that a result that comes with the call does not make it flash past, "Preparing the next task…" with the topic being picked; then the `coming` screen, in the same card. A call cancelled says the task was not finished, and no result two minutes after the ask says the task is taking longer than usual; both say a task can be asked for in the chat, and nothing on the card asks. Before its result the card knows no child, so it has no line at its top and no grade. It speaks the language the task was asked in, the call's `language` argument, which the host tells it with the call's arguments (8.4), so that the wait goes on in the words it began in once the result comes, unless the parent chose another language for the lessons (7.5); until the host has told it, the card shows nothing — no step and no word that the task is taking long —, since the host's own language may not be the lesson's, and only a call cancelled is said, in the host's language, as nothing comes after it (R144, R190). A card drawn for any other tool — `submit_task` from an earlier list of the tools among them — draws nothing before its result, and a result with no payload that is no failure, as `next_task` answered before R152, draws nothing at all.

`result` is drawn by `show_result`, on a card of its own, for an answer given in the chat. `submit_answer`, which the card calls itself, draws no card and carries no `ui.resourceUri` (03-flows): only a tool the model calls draws a new card, so the task's card turns into how its answer went itself, from that call's own payload, and nothing goes to the chat (R97, R286).

`progress` is drawn by `get_progress`, which carries `ui.resourceUri`, and is also reached inside a task card from its top line, which reads the same payload with `read_progress`; "Back to task" returns to the card as it was (R91). Until the reply is in, the card shows the screen's outline under the child's line — a faint shape wherever its words and marks will stand, with a sheen passing over the outline, and a bar that runs under the way back — and a status tells a screen reader the progress is loading; nothing moves for a reader who asks for no motion (R268). A progress card the model's own `get_progress` drew has no task behind it, so it has no top line at all: nothing to go back to, and the progress is already open.

## 8.3 What the widget does

| Action | How |
|---|---|
| Record an answer | `callServerTool("submit_answer", …)` — the result comes back to the widget, which turns into how the answer went in the task's place, from the result's own payload, tells the model the answer's line (below), and asks the chat for nothing (R286). Under how it went are Another task and, once the trial series is over, the choice of the topic, which ask the chat as before an answer (R252). A focus lost with the option pressed goes to Another task, and a screen reader hears the verdict. An answer recorded before is shown as it was recorded, and says so; a reply from an earlier service, which told the result alone, is shown from the task on the card, without the picture of the solution and the link to the topic. The card sends a letter alone: "I don't know" is said in the chat, where the model records it with `answer: "?"` (R93, R208) |
| Show the progress | `callServerTool("read_progress")`, from the line at the top of the card; it only reads, and its payload — the same as `get_progress`'s — carries the profile's fields the progress screen shows, so one call draws the whole screen (R91). It carries no `ui.resourceUri` and is hidden from the model, so no host has a card to draw for it (R97) |
| Wait for the task asked for | `callServerTool("read_task", {request_id})`, on the card `next_task` drew (R152), and `{request_id, refused}` once the card has heard the task is being written, so that the service may hold the question for news (R259): a moment after it is drawn — a moment of the request's own, under 1.5 s, so that the cards of a chat drawn again do not all ask at once —, then every 4 s while the task is written, every 15 s and the request's own moment more once two minutes have passed with no news or after a question that went unanswered, so that cards held back together do not ask again together; each pause counted from the start of the question before, so that a question held for news is followed at once, and from the end of one that went unanswered; one question at a time, none while the page is out of sight and one at once when it is looked at again. Two questions held for news that go unanswered in a row — a host may cut a call that takes a while — turn the holding off for the card's life. It stops once the task is on the card, once two answers in a row say none is coming — a read straight after a write may bring the file as it was before it, so the first such answer is shown and a later one can take it back —, or once twenty questions in a row have gone unanswered. It only reads, carries no `ui.resourceUri` and is hidden from the model |
| Ask for the next task | `sendMessage` (`ui/message`), from a task's card, before its answer or under how it went, or from the card of how an answer went — only the model can write a task, and `next_task` draws the card it comes to (R252, R286). The card sends the button's own label, in the card's language, as the adult's message (R131, R261), keeps its task, and says that once the ask reaches the chat the new task will come below, in a card of its own. While the ask is on its way the button takes no second press and the options none, since an answer would race the ask. Once the host has it, the card is done with: its options stay locked, its buttons and its choice of the topic are gone, and what it says of the new task takes the focus. A host may hold the message for the person to send, and the card cannot see whether it went, but a second ask would skip the task the new card brings. A refusal by the host is said on the card, which is given back to ask again (R145, R252) |
| Choose the topic | `callServerTool("edit_profile", {lesson_topic})` from the choice the topic's button opens under the task's row, or from the cross of the mark above a task given on the topic chosen (3.6, R194): the topic's id, or the empty text for the coach's choice. The choice offers the coach's choice, "by the child's progress"; "Suggested" — the review's topics the payload names, up to three —; and every topic of the catalog in the groups of the site's page of topics, in its order, each group's name a link to its part of that page, `/<language>/topics/#<group>`, built and opened as a topic's page is (the row below). The groups come with the build from the site's data, and the lowest grade of each topic from the catalog; a topic whose lowest grade is above the child's is quieter and says from which grade, and can be chosen all the same, since the rule does not read the grade. The choice in force is pressed (`aria-pressed`) and ticked: the choices are buttons, since a choice asks for a task at once, and the arrows of a group of radio buttons would choose at every step. The button names the choice (`aria-controls`) and says whether it is open (`aria-expanded`), as the hint's does; Escape and the cross close it; the focus goes to the choice in force as it opens, and back to the button as it closes. A choice saved is told to the model in the service's words with `updateModelContext`, when it changed anything — after the line of an answer given on the card that no message has carried yet, since the host keeps one line —, and asked for as another task is once the host has taken the line, or half a second later, in the adult's words — "Another task — on the topic “Clocks”", or "Another task — the coach chooses the topic" —, after which the card is done with as after another task, or, where the host refuses the ask, given back with the button naming the new choice; one not saved is said under the choice, and nothing goes to the chat. While a choice is on its way the card is locked as it is while an answer is checked — no answer, no hint, no other ask and no other choice —; while an answer is checked or an ask is on its way, it takes no choice. A card whose task is closed has neither its foot nor the mark |
| Change the profile | `callServerTool("edit_profile", …)` from the form "Edit" opens, with the fields that changed and no other, so that a change the model made meanwhile is kept (R148). The card shows the details saved at once, says "Saved." — and, when the language changed, that the new one comes with the next task, the card going on in its own words — and tells the model the service's words of the change with `updateModelContext`. A refusal is said under each field it names, by its code (5.9); a save that failed says to try again; a profile gone says so and offers no form. While a save is on its way the form takes no second one, and its fields are locked |
| Create the profile | Nothing: the profile is made in the chat, and the first sign-in only says how (R148) |
| Tell the model what happened | `updateModelContext` after an answer — one line, no conversation turn spent, which the model reads with the adult's next message: which task, which choice, whether it was right and the right option (R131), whether the mistake repeats (R136), and that the card shows how it went, so that the model calls no `show_result` for it and says nothing of the answer unless the adult asks, and then adds to the card rather than retells it (R286) — after a change on the profile's form, the service's own words of it, the notes left out (R148); and after a choice of the topic, the service's words of that (R194). A line replaces the one before it |
| Open a topic's page | `openLink` (`ui/open-link`), when a topic's name is pressed on the progress — in Topics, to the top of its page, and in a step of the review, to the part the step is about: `#traps` for a trap's advice, the top of the page for a topic to begin, `#home` for any other. A name is a link only on a host that said, as it connected, that it opens links (8.4), and only for a page the catalog marks published; elsewhere it is plain text. The card builds the address itself — `site`'s origin, https and nothing more, its own language when the site is written in it and English otherwise, and the topic's slug —, never takes one from the payload whole, and does not follow the link itself: the host decides, and may ask the person first. The middle button asks as a press does; the browser's own menu opens the address as it opens any link's, where the host lets it. A host that refuses, fails or never answers leaves the address under the name, as text to copy, left to right in every language, in a place under the name a screen reader is told of from the start, and the link stays to try again; a press while the host is still answering asks nothing. The profile's file in Drive is named and never opened: it is no page of the site (R135, R186) |
| Fit its card | `sendSizeChanged` when the content's height changes |

It calls no other tool: the answer, a change on the profile's form and the choice of the topic are the three things it writes — the last two with `edit_profile` —, and the rest — the progress and how the task it waits for stands — it only reads (R148, R194). No `requestDisplayMode` — a task card is an inline card. No `downloadFile`. The hint needs no call at all: it arrives with the task and is revealed locally, and the fact that it was opened travels with the answer (03-flows).

`sendMessage` and `updateModelContext` were confirmed in T03 only as far as "the call returns"; both have compensating controls and neither is load-bearing for correctness (03-flows).

## 8.4 What the host tells the widget

The app context arrives at startup and again on every change (`ui/notifications/host-context-changed`). What v1 uses, with the values T03 saw live in Claude:

| Field | Used for | Live values |
|---|---|---|
| `locale` | The dictionary (8.6) | `en-US` on web and phone |
| `theme` | Light or dark | `dark` on web, `light` on the phone — it follows the device, not the account |
| `displayMode` | Layout; `inline` is the only one v1 designs for | `inline` |
| `containerDimensions` | Nothing: the card measures its own width, which is the container's, and takes the wide layout from 640 px (R131) | 736 px on web, 353 px on the phone |
| `safeAreaInsets` | The page's padding at the screen's sides, left and right, not mirrored for a language written right to left (R131). The room above and below is not the card's: a host grows the bottom one while a reply comes in under an inline card, and as padding it became empty height under the card that the chat scrolled to (R287) | All zeros for an inline card with the host's border (T03); without it, a bottom one that grows under a reply (R287) |
| `styles` | Nothing: the card's own palette and fonts decide (8.12.1), and the host decides only light or dark (R131) | `light-dark(…)` variables |
| `timeZone` | Nothing yet — see below | IANA format |
| `toolInfo` | Which tool the card was drawn for, by the end of its name, since a host may put a prefix of its own before it, each host its own way (R07): a card drawn for `next_task` shows the wait for the task asked for before the result (R152). The bridge keeps the name itself, since the handshake's answer replaces the context | The whole tool definition, `_meta` included, in a change of context (T03) |

Besides the context, the host tells a card of its call: `ui/notifications/tool-input` once the arguments have come whole, `tool-input-partial` while they come, and `tool-cancelled` when the call is cancelled. The widget takes that the call was cancelled and, of the arguments told whole before the result, the language they name and nothing else, which the card of a task asked for speaks before its result (7.5); the arguments still coming, whose language may be cut short, it does not listen for, and the library drops them. Nothing more of the arguments is read: a task handed in carries its answer in them, and a host with an earlier list of the tools still draws a card for that call (R152, R190). The library holds a host to sending the whole arguments once, after the handshake and before the result; whether Claude does is checked live (T64.5). Claude draws a card's frame as its call starts ([the T62 report](docs/live/07-acceptance-claude.md), finding 4), which is why the card of a task is drawn by `next_task`, the call the model makes at once; a card learns nothing of a call that did not draw it, which is why it asks `read_task` (R152), and why the arguments of `submit_task` still being typed show no card how the task is coming along (R259).

The handshake's answer names what the host can do as well, `hostCapabilities`. The card reads one of them, `openLinks`: where the host names it, a topic's name on the progress is a link the card asks the host to open (8.3); where it does not, the name is plain text, since a link the host would not open is a button that does nothing (R186).

The layout works from **320 px** and never scrolls horizontally (PRODUCT 4.2); 353 px is what a real phone gave us, so the margin is thin and is tested rather than assumed. The `platform` the host names is not read either: the card follows its own width, and keeps hover colours to pointers that can hover (R131).

`timeZone` is the field that answers an open question from 03-flows: the daily counters roll over at a UTC midnight because the server has no idea where the family is, and the host has been telling the widget all along. v1 keeps UTC — the counters are cost ceilings and nothing is displayed from them (R13) — and so does the week the progress tells a change over (2.10): the time zone reaches the widget alone, while the service works the change out, and the two tools of the progress take no arguments; a zone the widget sent with `read_progress` would make it answer what `get_progress` does not (R97, R165). If that ever changes, the timezone does not need asking for.

## 8.5 When there is no widget

Text mode is not a fallback path through different tools; it is the same tools with nobody drawing the cards (О-10). Every result's `content` is a complete rendering of that screen in words: the task read out without its answer, the diagnosis after an answer, the progress as a short list. A host that renders nothing loses the button that records an answer without spending a conversation turn, and the pictures: there is no picture in words, so the question carries every fact the task needs and the picture only shows what it says, and the solution says what the picture of the solution shows (4.4, R228, R269, R276). `show_result` there tells how the answer went in words, as `submit_answer`'s words do (7.3, R278). That is the whole of what it loses.

The one thing text mode must never do is improvise the missing pieces: the answer is not in the payload to be read out early, and the model has no way to fetch it before the child answers.

## 8.6 Dictionaries

Strings are never baked into a component; they are looked up by key (PRODUCT 4.2).

- **Format** — one flat JSON object per locale, `web/locales/<tag>.json`, the file named by its tag as `Intl.Locale` writes it (`pt`, `zh-Hans`). Keys are in dot notation, the first word the screen or the speaker the words belong to (`task.hint`, `result.wrong`, `waiting.step.answers`, `progress.rank`). The catalogs' topics and skills are named by their ids, `topic.<id>` and `skill.<id>`, which a test holds every id of the catalogs to; a name the card has no words for is shown as its id (R135). A picture is named for a screen reader by its kind, `picture.<kind>` — "A clock face", "Things in a row" (R272). Placeholders are named in lowercase letters: `{count}`, `{grade}`, `{topic}`. A number put into one is written the way the language writes numbers, through `Intl.NumberFormat` — a rating alone with no separator between its thousands, 1573, as chess writes one, in the language's digits (R131).
- **Plurals** — `Intl.PluralRules` with the CLDR categories, so a key that varies by number is a small object naming exactly the categories its language has — `{"one": "…", "few": "…", "many": "…", "other": "…"}` in Russian, `{"one": "…", "other": "…"}` in English — and the text is chosen by the placeholder `{count}`. Dates, when a screen shows one, go through `Intl.DateTimeFormat`. No i18n library, no ICU parser: the platform has all three.
- **Where they live** — inside the single HTML file, all of them. The widget has no network (8.1), so a dictionary it does not already hold is a dictionary it can never fetch. The dictionaries grew with the screens, to 189 keys and 10 to 17 KB of JSON a language — the scripts of India and Thailand take three bytes a letter — so the twenty-two of v1 took the page from 323 KB to 543 KB, 151 KB compressed (T59). The page went on growing with its screens, to 830 KB, 221 KB compressed with `gzip -9`, before pictures; the pictures added 43 KB, 15 KB compressed — their twelve drawings and the layout of their words 9 KB of it, the card's reading of a picture 2 KB, and a name for each kind in every language 4 KB — to 873 KB, 236 KB compressed (R272). No budget is set for the page. If its size ever matters, the escape is to inline one locale per resource read rather than to give the widget a network.
- **Lookup** — the languages wanted, most wanted first: the one the parent chose for the cards, when a payload carries it, then the host's `locale` (7.5). Each is tried with its full tag (`pt-BR`), then shorter (`pt`), then with the script its language is usually written in, so that the `zh-CN` a host sends finds `zh-Hans` — but never cut past its script, so that `zh-TW` is not given the simplified characters a bare `zh` stands for. The first dictionary found is spoken, and `en` when there is none. A language no tag names is never tried: a host in Kazakh gets English, not Russian, however many who read the one read the other, and an undetermined `und-RU` gets English too (R129). A missing key falls back the same way — the dictionary's shorter tag in its script, then `en` — and, in a development build and in the widget's tests, fails loudly; so does a placeholder left empty, and a plural key given no count.
- **Checked** — the keys a component names are typed from `en.json`, so a key English lacks does not compile. A test holds every dictionary to the keys of English, each wording to the placeholders of its English one and to braces that are placeholders alone, each plural wording to the categories of its language, and each file's name to the tag `Intl.Locale` writes. The layout is measured too: every state of a lesson the preview draws, in every language and in the pseudo-language below, at 320, 360 and 640 px, in Chromium and in WebKit, fails when its page scrolls sideways, a part of the card sticks out of it, or text runs out of its box or is cut short — all but the pseudonym at the top, which gives way to what stands beside it — and when a task's picture is wider than the room it stands in, or writes a word past its own edges, over another word, or smaller on the screen than 11 px or than the picture writes it (`just web-layout`, R140, R272). From T66b on, a pull request measures three languages — the pseudo-language, English and one drawn at random from the rest — and main all of them (R281).
- **The pseudo-language** — a development build, the preview and the widget's tests among them, speaks one language more: `en-XA`, the tag Android gives its own, English with every letter accented, every vowel said twice, and counting words added until each text is twice as long as its English one and at least ten characters longer, set in brackets, its placeholders and plural forms as they were. That is longer than the twenty-two dictionaries say all but two of their keys, and those two by a character or two: measured when they were written, a text of twenty characters or more grows to at most 1.95 times its English, and a label of a word or two to at most 3.3 times. A card that holds the pseudo-language holds longer words than its languages give it today. The build that ships leaves it out, since a host could name it.
- **Adding a language** is adding a file (PRODUCT 4.2); the card needs no code changes. The service's checks may: a language written in a script the language check does not know, or in several scripts, is held to nothing until the check is taught it (5.11), a language that ends a sentence with a mark the split does not know is measured wrong (5.5), and a language whose words for "and", "or" and "with" the check of a picture's labels does not hold is read with every language's words (5.4). The checks know the sixty-four languages of 8.7 (R285), and a test refuses a dictionary in a language the language check holds no question of. The words of v1 started as those of the design the widget was ported from (R89, 8.12.1), in `en` and `ru`, and T59 added the other twenty languages of 8.7; T66b adds the forty-two of R275.

## 8.7 The languages of v1

О-14а asks for the world's largest languages. v1's list is **64**: `en`, `zh-Hans`, `hi`, `es`, `ar`, `fr`, `bn`, `pt`, `ru`, `ur`, `id`, `de`, `ja`, `tr`, `ko`, `vi`, `it`, `fa`, `pl`, `uk`, `th`, `nl`, and the forty-two the author added on 2026-10-10 (R275): `pa`, `te`, `mr`, `ta`, `gu`, `fil`, `sw`, `ms`, `ro`, `cs`, `hu`, `el`, `sv`, `da`, `nb`, `fi`, `uz`, `kk`, `kn`, `ml`, `or`, `mai`, `ps`, `he`, `az`, `ku`, `ckb`, `ha`, `yo`, `ig`, `am`, `zu`, `sr`, `hr`, `bs`, `bg`, `sk`, `ca`, `zh-Hant`, `lt`, `hy`, `ka`. A language with a choice of script or standard is written in the one CLDR gives its bare tag. Punjabi is in Gurmukhi, so a host's `pa-PK`, which stands for Shahmukhi, gets English; Serbian is in Cyrillic, so `sr-Latn` gets English; Norwegian is Bokmål, `nb`; Uzbek and Azerbaijani are in Latin and Kazakh in Cyrillic. Kurdish has two dictionaries, Kurmanji `ku` in Latin and Sorani `ckb` in Arabic. Filipino is `fil`, the tag `Intl.Locale` writes for `tl` too. Every language of the list is meant for the cards and the lessons. The site speaks nine of them, the first tier of R281 — `en`, `es`, `pt`, `de`, `fr`, `ja`, `ru`, `zh-Hans` and `ar` —, and any other joins it only in the order R281 gives. The forty-two are not written yet: T66b writes them, and until then the cards speak the first twenty-two.

The list is a starting point chosen by number of speakers and by where a parent might plausibly meet the app, not a promise. A locale outside it is served by its language's nearest match or by English, which is exactly what the lookup above does.

**Who wrote which words.** Not every dictionary is as trustworthy as the next, and a card does not say so, so this is where it is said:

| Dictionary | Its words |
|---|---|
| `en` | The source: every key is written here first, and the words of the design the widget was ported from were the first (R89) |
| `ru` | Written in Russian beside the English, from that design's Russian page and for every key since — not translated from the English by a machine |
| The other twenty: `zh-Hans`, `hi`, `es`, `ar`, `fr`, `bn`, `pt`, `ur`, `id`, `de`, `ja`, `tr`, `ko`, `vi`, `it`, `fa`, `pl`, `uk`, `th`, `nl` | **Machine translations, not yet read by anyone who speaks the language** — written in T59 from the English, with the Russian beside it and a note for every key on who reads it, where it stands and how short it has to be: the informal "you" to the child and the polite one to the parent, nothing that shows the child's gender, the ranks as places rather than titles (R140) — since T81 the ranks are told by their numbers, and have no names to translate (R213) |
| The forty-two of R275: `pa`, `te`, `mr`, `ta`, `gu`, `fil`, `sw`, `ms`, `ro`, `cs`, `hu`, `el`, `sv`, `da`, `nb`, `fi`, `uz`, `kk`, `kn`, `ml`, `or`, `mai`, `ps`, `he`, `az`, `ku`, `ckb`, `ha`, `yo`, `ig`, `am`, `zu`, `sr`, `hr`, `bs`, `bg`, `sk`, `ca`, `zh-Hant`, `lt`, `hy`, `ka` | **Not written yet.** T66b writes them as machine translations, made the way the twenty were, and this row then says so |

A dictionary a speaker of its language has read moves to a row of its own, with who read it and when. Until then a wrong word is a bug like any other, and mending it is editing one file.

## 8.8 Right to left

`ar`, `fa` and `ur` are written right to left, and so will `ps`, `he` and `ckb` be once T66b writes them (R275), so the widget sets `dir="rtl"` on the document for them and lays out with logical CSS properties — `margin-inline-start`, `padding-inline-end`, `text-align: start` — rather than left and right. The direction is read from the script the dictionary's tag names or its language is usually written in — Arabic, Hebrew, Thaana and the other scripts written right to left — not from a list of languages, so a dictionary added later is laid out right with no code. With it the document gets `lang`, the dictionary's tag, so that a screen reader reads the card in the language its words are in (R129). The task's own words — its question, options, hint, trap and solution — are in the language the task was written in, and carry that tag and its direction themselves (R131).

The markup of eight screens in Arabic — the task, the result of a wrong answer, the wait for a task being handed in, a try that did not pass, the progress, the profile, the first sign-in and the profile's form refused field by field — is held to snapshots in the widget's tests, and the layout check photographs every card in `ar`, `fa` and `ur` at a phone's width, for the eye: on the main branch, and on a pull request only where its commit picks one of the three (R140, R282).

One exception matters more than the rest: **the picture is always laid out left to right**. A number line, a row or a balance means what it means by where its parts stand; mirroring it turns a correct picture into a wrong one. The picture's SVG sets the `direction` property to `ltr`, as an attribute and in its style: `dir` is HTML's and an SVG element does not read it, while the text inside the SVG inherits the document's right-to-left direction. In the study's Arabic cards, with `dir` alone, the notes under the bars ran out of the picture (R269). The wording around it is mirrored, the picture is not. The card lays a picture out in the room a card of 320 px gives it — 286 px, less 2 px on either side for the strokes along its edges — so that it is drawn at its own size on every card, and it writes no word smaller than 11 px, but for the widest labels of a table of five or six columns and of a grid of seven or eight, which it writes smaller to fit: four or five wide capitals to a cell, which none of the reference pictures has. A picture that is wider still shrinks to the card's width, and never scrolls (4.4, R131, R272).

## 8.9 Server-rendered pages

The pages of the sign-in are rendered by the server rather than by a widget: the consent screen of 02-auth, the page that asks a parent who left Google's box for the Drive unticked to go back and tick it (9.3), and the error pages of the sign-in flow, among them the one a parent's browser is shown past the sign-in's pace (10.3). They exist outside the chat, in a browser, with no app context to read a locale from.

They are served in **English alone**, whatever language the browser reads, with `Content-Language: en` on the response (О-51, R139). Their words are `internal/transport/oauth/pages/en.json`, one flat object as 8.6 has it, and a test holds it to every word the pages ask for and to no other. That is a deliberate narrowing of PRODUCT 4.2, which describes widgets: the consent screen is what a parent agrees to, and the terms and the privacy policy it points at, `/en/terms/` and `/en/privacy/`, are the texts that prevail in English wherever a translation of them disagrees (О-50). A consent screen in the language of the texts it accepts is one wording of the agreement rather than twenty-two to keep in step with them; the page a sign-in stops at is the other half of the same flow, and goes with it.

## 8.10 `window.openai`

ChatGPT exposes its own `window.openai` API beside the standard one: widget state, modal windows, payment (PRODUCT 9.3). v1 uses none of it. The widget is written against the MCP Apps library alone, and where a host offers something extra it is read through feature detection and treated as sugar — never as a requirement, and never as a second code path to test. The same rule covers anything ChatGPT-specific that turns up in T63: if it cannot be feature-detected and ignored, it does not go in. The server sends two keys of ChatGPT's own with the widget's resource, `openai/widgetDomain` and `openai/widgetCSP` (8.1): a key a host does not know is one it reads past, and the widget itself reads neither (R216).

## 8.11 Every scenario, its tools and its screens

The acceptance check for this part: each scenario of PRODUCT 3 has tools, a screen and a rendering in words.

| Scenario | Tools | Screens | In text mode |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. First sign-in | `next_task`, which says there is no profile, then `save_profile` | `first_run`, drawn by `next_task` and by any other tool that draws a card and meets no profile; it only tells (R148, R152) | The model asks the adult to say they are the child's parent or tutor, then for the pseudonym, grade, interests and constraints in the chat, and says in a sentence what was saved |
| 2. The task | `next_task`, `get_package`, `submit_task`; `read_task`, called by the card; then `prepare_task` and `submit_task` again, for the next task, written ahead and kept (R235) | the wait as the task is asked for → `coming` → `task`, in one card that asks how the task stands (R152); a task written ahead and kept is `task` at once | The model gets the package, writes the task and hands it in; the wording and the options `A`–`E` are read out, with no picture, since the wording carries every fact (8.5); the hint on request; then it writes the next task ahead and says nothing about it |
| 3. The answer | `submit_answer`, called by the card (R286) | `task` → how the answer went, in the task's place on the same card (R286) | The adult types the child's letter, the model calls the tool and explains from the trap it returns, about the step, for the adult to read out, and the solution step by step; when the child does not know, the adult says so, which is recorded with `?`, a wrong answer, and gets the solution (R93, R261) |
| 4. The next task | `sendMessage` → `next_task`, which puts the task written ahead on the new card at once, then `prepare_task`, `submit_task`, which get the one after it ready; with none kept, `next_task`, `get_package`, `submit_task`; a topic chosen on the card is saved with `edit_profile` first (3.6, R194, R235, R252) "Another task" pressed on the card of how the answer went, or on a task's card before its answer, or the choice of the topic under their buttons, that card then done with; then a new card: the task written ahead at once → `task`, or the wait → `coming` → `task` (R145, R152, R252) | The adult asks in words — for a topic to keep to, too, which the model saves with `save_profile` (3.6) —, and the task written ahead is read out at once |
| 5. Progress | `get_progress`, called by the model, or `read_progress`, called by the widget from a task card (R91, R97) | `progress` | The overall rating with its rank and how far through it, each topic met with its rating and rank, ahead of or behind the overall one, the topics within reach not met yet, mastered topics, the latest tasks and how many were left without an answer in all, the recommendation and where the profile's file is, as a short list, and the review of the topics for the adult — the strong ones, the ones to develop and why, the ones too early to judge and what to do next, each step with its advice (2.11); asked what to work on, the model calls `get_progress` and tells the adult the review; during the trial series, how many of its five tasks are done instead of the ratings and the review |
| 6. The profile | `get_profile` and `save_profile`, called by the model; `edit_profile`, called by the form in the progress's Profile section (R148, R162) | `progress`, its profile and the form "Edit" opens there | The same fields, read out and changed by asking; a change on the form reaches the model as a line |

Every row's text column is the `content` of the same result that draws the screen — one payload, two renderings (7.3).

## 8.12 The design

Two designs, each with a reference of its own (R156). The card — every screen of the widget — is drawn by its own code, and so is the site, ported from the author's draft with the product's facts wherever the two disagreed. They share the tokens' sizes; neither takes the other's look (R142, R157).

### 8.12.1 The card

**Its reference** is its code: the components in `web/src/design/` and the screens in `web/src/widget/`, seen in every state of a lesson, in both themes, in every language the widget speaks and at 320, 360 and 640 px, in `just web-preview` (R133). A change to the card's look is shown to the author there before it ships; `just web-layout` measures it in two browsers, on the preview built as its server serves it, in every language on the main branch and in three on a pull request that touches a file the cards are drawn from (R140, R282), and `just web-screens` photographs the README's pictures to be looked at, while every release photographs its own and the README shows them from the branch `screens` (R150, R227). A screen being redrawn after a mockup follows the mockup until it is ported, as the progress followed `rework/3a-ranks.png` (R147) and now follows `draft/ui/profile/Рейтинг 4a.html` (R162) and its second version, `Рейтинг 4a v2.html`, for the ranks told by number and the grades marked under the course (R213), and the choice of the topic followed frame 5a of `draft/ui/next-task/topic-choice.html` (R194). The card was first ported from the author's static export, `draft/ui/mathtrail-site/` (R89), removed on 2026-10-03 once the widget matched it; it is in the repository's history up to `8ff72f8`.

**The tokens** — `internal/widget/tokens.css` (R94). Colours for the light and the dark theme, chosen by `data-theme` on the document — which is what the widget's bridge sets from the host's theme — and by `prefers-color-scheme` when nothing sets it, the ramp a topic's rank is drawn in among them (R147), and the green of a gain and the coral of a step back that a move is striped and written in (R171), and the faint colour of a screen being read and the sheen that passes over it (R268); the palette a picture paints with, the same in every theme as a flag's colours are, with the ink each paint's letters are written in (R277); spacing from 4 to 120 px; radii; the card's widths, 360 px narrow and 640 px wide; a tap target of 44 px and an option at least 48 px high; system font stacks only, since the widget loads nothing (8.1) and the policy of the pages the service renders admits no font (9.2). One file, shared by the widget, the site and the consent screen the service renders. The site takes its spaces, radii and widths from it, and its colours and its typeface from its own stylesheet (R142, R157); it never redefines `--font-sans`.

**The components** live in `web/src/design/`, every class under the `mt-` prefix, with the names, the classes and the states the design gave them:

| Component | What it is |
|---|---|
| `WidgetApp` | The whole card and its screens, in `web/src/widget/` |
| `ThreadBar`, `NameBar` | The line at the top: the child's pseudonym and "Profile & progress", or "Back to task"; on a card drawn where there is no progress to open — every card on the site — the pseudonym alone, with nothing to press (R197) |
| `MessageHeader`, `Badge`, `Mark` | Who speaks — MathTrail by its logo, the child by the avatar, an icon of the set — with the grade as a badge; the logo is drawn in the card, since a card loads nothing, once for each theme, and the theme's tokens show one: in the dark theme as the site's icon draws it, which a test holds it to (R191), and in the light theme the same ribbon and star on a white tile under a hairline, the palest of their inks deepened (R203); MathTrail's header at the top of a card closes its line with the version of the build, the release it runs (R154), in a frame of two parts: the word for a version in the card's language, quiet on a tinted ground, and the number without its "v" in the monospaced face — the word over the number on a narrow card, beside it on a wide one; the frame keeps its width, and a long number wraps inside it (R195); no `⋯` button, and the end padding that made room for it is the start's (remark 40) |
| `OptionList`, `OptionRow` | The five answers as buttons with their letters (R90), in the states default, selected (checking), correct, wrong and muted — buttons rather than radio inputs, whose arrow keys would give an answer by moving through them (R131) —, under a legend a screen reader alone hears (R263) |
| `Diagram` | The picture, `web/src/design/picture/`: a function for each kind draws the description as SVG, in the theme's colours through classes and the token `--picture-fill`, with no library and no style of its own, `role="img"` with a label that says what it shows, laid out left to right (4.4, 8.8, R269). Its words are placed by an estimate of how wide each is, the widest the fonts measured draw it, so that the site draws a picture at build time as the card draws it. It draws nothing for a task with no `picture`, and a picture that fails to draw is left out, and the task stands on its words (R272). A picture that paints is painted in the palette's tokens `--paint-*`, each paint carrying the fewest letters of its word that tell it from the others, and has a key under it, each colour's swatch with its letters beside its word, in the task's language (R277) |
| `Note` | A plain, hint or trap note |
| `Verdict`, `SolutionSteps`, `ResultCard` | How an answer went, `web/src/widget/ResultCard.tsx`: the verdict, the trap, the picture of the solution with the total under it, the solution in steps, and a band of the topic and the rating (R276, R278) — on a card of its own under the task's for an answer given in the chat, and in the task's place on a card answered (R286) |
| `GeneratingSteps` | The waiting screen's list of steps |
| `RankSummary`, `Segments`, `GradeLegend`, `GradeRuns`, `RankList`, `StatList`, `StatusMark`, `StatusDots`, `FieldsFrame`, `ProfileFields` | The progress: the rank with its course of steps and the grades marked under it, the topics each with theirs, lists with right and wrong marks and dots, a run of answers as dots, the profile's fields in frames (R147, R162, R213) |
| `MoveLine`, `MoveCounts`, `MoveLegend` | How the ranks moved: a course's steps striped by a pattern of their own, a line under the rank, the count of the topics that moved by their title, and a legend of the stripes, in the `--gain` and `--loss` tokens (R171) |
| `ViewSwitch` | A choice between views of the same thing, as a row of segments: radio buttons under a legend a screen reader alone hears — the while the progress draws its moves over (R171) |
| `Fold` | A part of a card folded away under its title, with a summary and an arrow: the sections of the progress (R162) |
| `LoadingBar`, `ProgressSkeleton` | A screen being read: a bar of the accent that runs along a track under the way back, and the progress's outline in the screen's own frames, a faint shape wherever its words and marks will stand, with a sheen passing over the outline; a status tells a screen reader it is loading, and nothing moves for a reader who asks for no motion (R268) |
| `ReviewPart`, `JudgedList`, `NamedLine`, `AdviceSteps` | The review of the topics: its parts under headings in the colour of what they say, the topics each after a dot with why under the name, a line of names with a note, and the steps numbered, each the topic it is for over what to do (R180) |
| `TextField`, `SelectField`, `ChipsField`, `CheckGroup`, `Checkbox` | The profile's form: a box to type in, the platform's own list, chips added and taken out, and ticks, each under its name with what is wrong with it said under it (R148) |
| `TopicButton`, `TopicPanel`, `TopicChip` | The choice of the topic on a task's card, in `web/src/widget/`: the button at the end of the row, in green, with a spark while the coach chooses and a tag once a topic is chosen — on a narrow card the topic alone, or "Coach" (R263) —, its words wrapping and its arrow turning up while the choice is open; the panel under the row, its choices pressed in while in force, each group's name in a column of 140 px beside its topics on a wide card and above them on a narrow one; and the mark above a task given on the topic chosen (R194) |
| `Button`, `IconButton`, `Icon` | Buttons — one that opens a part apart from it names that part, and one drawn by its icon alone is a finger's size, named by its words to a screen reader and under a pointer (R263) —, and MathTrail's own icons: one set on a grid of 24, in lines of one width, rounded and never filled, each in one colour — the text's, or the token of what it marks (a verdict, a step, the avatar); the site's menu and chat are icons of it (R191), and so are the two arrows in a circle, the spark and the tag of a task's buttons (R263) |

**The words** are the dictionary's (8.6); the first of them were the export's, in English and Russian. The grade is a slot, `{grade}`, wherever a text names it — the label under the child's name and the badge of the task card's header, "Olympiad coach · Grade 3" — never a number written into a string, since one child's grade is not another's. A card in a chat has no time of its own to show and no button that opens nothing (remark 40). A card on the site speaks the widget's own words in the page's language, since it is the widget's component; the data it is drawn from and the words around it — a caption, the chat's messages — are the site's (8.12.2, R170).

**What a card shows and does** is 8.2 and 8.3; this section fixes how it looks.

### 8.12.2 The site

**Its reference** is its code: the pages in `web/src/site/` and their words in `site/`, seen in the site `just site` builds (R156). They were ported from the author's draft, `draft/ui/site/`: six pages drawn in a design tool, each a self-unpacking HTML file that opens in a browser — the home page in English and Russian, and "Why", "Topics", a topic's page ("Knights and liars"), "Techniques" (R181) and "Research" in Russian alone. The draft fixed what the pages are, their sections and their order, the Russian words and the look; the English words were written anew from it and are the source. Once every page of it was ported, on 2026-10-06, it was removed, and it is in the history up to `66680ac`. "About" follows a draft of its own, which the author keeps outside the repository with the originals of its photographs: with its photographs, and without the children's names (R183, R200). "Coach" followed a draft of its own, `draft/ui/couch/`, removed once ported, whose prototype the page frames (R198), and follows a second, `draft/MathTrail - Тренер.html` of 2026-10-07, which sets a screen of that prototype beside the first screen's words and frames the very same prototype (R249). "Service" follows a draft of its own, `draft/MathTrail - Сервис.html` of 2026-10-07, drawn after the code was, whose words the page holds to the code where the two disagree (R251).

**The pages and their addresses** (R158):

| Page | Address | Its words | Task |
|---|---|---|---|
| Home: the promise, how a lesson goes, how to connect; the English one is the bare domain itself, and the language is switched in the header (R253) | `/` in English, `/<locale>/` in any other language | `index.yaml` | T66a.1, T66a.2 |
| Where the English home page was served before the bare domain: sends the reader on to `/` at once, loading no stylesheet, font or picture, and names it as its canonical (R206, R253) | `/en/` | The English home page's title and line | T19, T66 |
| The coach, a product of the author's own still in the making, and its prototype to try | `/<locale>/coach/` | `coach.yaml` | T75.14 |
| Why olympiad maths, what research has found about it, and why MathTrail | `/<locale>/why/` | `why.yaml` | T75.10 |
| The topics, and how they link | `/<locale>/topics/` | `topics.yaml` | T75.4 |
| A topic of the catalog | `/<locale>/topics/<slug>/` | `topics/<slug>.yaml` | T75.5–T75.9 |
| The techniques of problem solving, each worked through on a problem | `/<locale>/techniques/` | `techniques.yaml` | T75.12 |
| Research: the numbers behind the product, every one from data (`docs/research-page.md`, R204, R221, R250; its draft is `research/draft-ui/`) | `/<locale>/research/` | `research.yaml` | T72.12, T72.13, T72.15 |
| Service: how the service works — its parts and what they talk to, a lesson call by call, where its state lives, the profile's file and what is sealed; a technical page, held with "Research" in the menu (R251) | `/<locale>/service/` | `service.yaml` | T75.15 |
| About: who makes MathTrail, and where to write | `/<locale>/about/` | `about.yaml` | T75.11, T75.13 |
| Help: what a family needs, how to connect, how a lesson goes, what to do when something looks wrong, and where to write; the directories' listings name it as the support page and the documentation (R229) | `/<locale>/help/` | `help.md` | T71.4.1 |
| The privacy policy and the terms of use | `/<locale>/privacy/`, `/<locale>/terms/` | `privacy.md`, `terms.md` | T19a, T61 |

A page's words are in `site/content/<locale>/`, and its name is the same English word in every language: the file's path without its extension, `topics/<slug>` for a topic. A published address never stops answering: one whose page moved, as `/en/` did when the English home page took the bare domain, keeps a page that sends its reader on to where the page is now (R253). The site's checker holds the list by hand (`web/tools/sitecheck/published.ts`), with the anchors linked from outside their page among them — every topic's `#traps` and `#home`, the anchor on "Topics" of every group a topic's path leads to, and the home page's `#lesson` and `#connect`, which the English one has at `/` (R158, R172, R253). Every page the site serves is on the list, and the checker refuses one that is not, so a page joins it with the change that publishes it (R167).

**The languages** are English and Russian until T66b translates the site into seven more, and then the nine of the first tier: `en`, `es`, `pt`, `de`, `fr`, `ja`, `ru`, `zh-Hans` and `ar` (8.7; О-57; R281). The build refuses a language of the site the widget does not speak: its cards, and the names its pages give topics and traps in the card's words, would be in another language (R172). Every page exists in every language of the site or in none (R158). The English text prevails where a translation differs; the privacy policy and the terms say so in their last section, "Languages", and no page carries a note that it is a translation (R177). The language is chosen by address: the switch links a page to the same page in each of the site's other languages.

**The home page** (R188) is drawn by `web/src/site/HomePage.tsx` from its words and from the site's data, `site/data.json` under `home`. Its parts follow the draft:
- the first screen: "Free", "Open source" and the catalog's grades; the promise, word for word as the README gives it; "Add to Claude", which leads to connecting, and a button to see a lesson; where it works today; and beside them, in a phone, the card of a task just handed out, in the chat a parent already uses, under the parent's message (R262);
- why not just ask the chat: three pillars — tasks that do not run out, a program that checks the model, a wrong answer that is a diagnosis — the last naming three traps of the catalog, which the data names, under the names the card gives them;
- `#lesson`, how a lesson goes: six steps, four numbered and two within the one before, each beside the widget's own card as a chat draws it at that step — the task being written, an option picked and being checked, the hint open, the wrong answer told with its trap and the solution, the question the adult asks in the chat once the answer is in, and the progress with its topics and its repeating mistakes open;
- `#connect`, how to connect: Claude's five steps, as Claude's help gives them, the first leading to Claude's connectors and the address after the third, written in full and selected whole by a click; ChatGPT's directory still to come and its developer mode meanwhile, leading to OpenAI's own description; and the accounts it takes;
- the last call: "Add to Claude", and the way to the code.

The lesson's card is a variant of the reference task `gaps-12-d5-4` — posts every 3 metres along a 12-metre fence — whose facts are the site's data: its topic and grade, its picture, its five options and the right one, the catalog's trap behind each wrong option, the option the steps pick, and the rating before an answer, after a wrong one and after a right one. Its words — the child's name, the task, the hint, the text of the trap behind each wrong option and the solution — are the page's. Its picture is a row of posts cut short — two posts, a gap of 3 and the rest skipped — under the fence's length, never all the posts, which would show the answer; it holds no words, and is the same in every language. The card on "Why" and the problem beside a school sum on its first screen show the same picture, drawn by the card's own function. The site has no text drawing: a topic's card on "Topics", the first screen and the examples of a topic's page are drawn from the site's data, as a picture or in markup where no kind fits (R181, R272, R274). The build refuses a wrong option with no trap behind it, a trap behind the right option, one the catalog lacks or one tied to no option, an example trap the catalog lacks, a wrong option whose trap the page says nothing of, a right answer that does not raise the rating or a wrong one that does not lower it, and every card "Why" refuses.

**The home page's demo** (R197) is one module, `web/src/demo/`, built into `/assets/demo.js`, which the home page names itself and alone on the site runs. The page carries what it needs, so that it loads nothing else: a block of JSON a browser never runs, `<script type="application/json" data-demo>`, with the page's language, the widget's words in that language alone, the lesson's task and what the service would record of every answer to it — each of its five options — read at build time through the widget's own reader. The demo:
- puts in place of the still card on the first screen the widget's own card of the lesson's task, drawn live and pressable: an option is checked for a moment and told as the data says, the hint opens, and "Another task" is the parent's message in the chat's frame, under it the card a task is written on, and then the lesson's task again; the page answers for the service and for the host, and calls, sends and keeps nothing;
- on a window 992 px wide and 700 px tall or more, where the first screen held stands whole under the menu, holds it in place while the page's scroll moves the chat in its phone, one to one, through to the chat's end and then on, idle, for half the window, before the page scrolls on; the page and the chat move together both ways — a chat the focus or the browser's search scrolls brings the page along, and a chat that changes size under an answer or another task, while the first screen is in sight, stays where it stands with the first screen held around it — and elsewhere the chat scrolls by itself (R262);
- on a window of 960 px and wider gives the steps of a lesson one card, in a column beside them that stays in sight: the cards the build drew for the steps, moved there and never copied, the one of the step at the middle of the window shown, fading as the next comes, at once for a reader who asks for less motion; below that width every step keeps its own card;
- puts beside the connector's address, in its pill, the button that copies it, kept in a template until then, where the browser can write to the clipboard; it says for a moment that it copied, and selects the address when the copy is refused.

**The topics' pages and their map** (1.2, R164). A topic's page lives at `/<locale>/topics/<slug>/`, its slug the catalog's. Every topic's page carries two anchors that other pages and the card link to — `#traps`, where children go wrong, and `#home`, how to help at home — and both join the list of published addresses with the page. A topic has a page when its `site_page` is on, and then in every language of the site; the site's build refuses a page without the flag and a flag without the page (T75.4, T75.5). The site's languages are those of its dictionaries, `web/src/site/locales/`. The card links a topic to its page only when `site_page` is on, in the card's own language when the site has it and in English otherwise (8.3, R186). The map on "Topics" draws the catalog's links: a topic's layer is the longest chain of bases below it — 0 the foundations, 1 the rules, 2 the hard problems — computed rather than drawn, and a test on the catalog keeps the chains within three layers (1.2). The map is laid out when the site is built (R170): a column for each layer, from left to right in every language as a drawing is, the foundations in the catalog's order and every later column by where its topics' bases stand; a link to the next column is a curve, and a link that skips one runs under every box, as the draft draws it. Pointing at a topic, or reaching it with the keyboard, lights every topic below it and above it and the lines between them, and says the same under the map, by rules the page's head carries, written from the catalog's links: no script runs, and a browser that cannot read the rules shows the map unlit. On a narrow screen the map scrolls sideways inside its panel. Each topic's card on the page says its links in words — what it builds on and what it opens — beside its drawing, a line for the parent and its grades, and leads to the topic's page once `site_page` is on, saying "soon" until then. The topics are shown in groups that are the site's data, `site/data.json`, every topic in exactly one; a group's name is a word of the site's dictionaries, since a topic's page names its group too. A topic's page names the topics it builds on and the topics that build on it.

**A topic's page** (R172) is drawn for every topic by one template, `web/src/site/TopicPage.tsx`, from the topic's words and from facts that are no words. Its parts follow the draft:
- the path "Topics › group › topic", whose group leads to the group's anchor on "Topics";
- the first screen: the group, the grades, the topic's name as the card says it, what the topic is, its main move and what it teaches, beside the topic's drawing;
- a list that leads to the parts below;
- the ground the topic stands on, as cards (the knights' island rules), and its key idea, as a table and a note;
- how such tasks are solved: the steps of the move, then examples worked through to their answers, from the simplest to an olympiad's, each with its task, its steps, its answer, and a drawing and a note where it needs them;
- `#traps`, where children go wrong: the topic's three traps most frequent in its reference tasks, under the names the card gives them, each with how it shows in the topic and what to say;
- `#home`, how to help at home, tip by tip;
- the topics to learn first and those the topic opens, from the catalog's links, each leading to its page once it is published and saying "soon" until then;
- how to ask for a task on it: the words to write in a chat, "Add to Claude", which leads to the home page's section on connecting, and the way back to every topic.

The words are a closed set of blocks: the template reads every key it knows, and the build refuses a key the file lacks and a text the page never shows; an example's note is the only part a page may leave out. The labels every topic's page shares are the site's dictionaries', and the names of a topic and of a trap the widget's. The traps are counted, not chosen: each wrong option of the topic's reference tasks that names a trap counts once, at every level, and ties go to the catalog's order — the count the service makes of a topic's usual mistakes for a new child (R67), taken over the whole topic; the file holds words for exactly the first three. The grades are the catalog's: the first screen says the topic's, and each example says the level it is set at, which the site's data holds by the topic's id with the example's solver and answer. The build refuses an example of a page the catalog does not publish, one at a level the topic is not taught at, and words that work through another number of examples than the data gives. Each example is proved: its solver is a Starlark program of its own, `site/solvers/<topic>/<name>.star`, written from the topic's template (`content/solvers/`), and a Go test beside the data, `site/examples_test.go`, runs every one the way a submitted solver is run — the same sandbox, the same limits, two runs under rotated labels — on its answer and four texts no answer is, and holds it to the answer; a solver no example names fails it. That the words, in every language, state the task the solver searches and the answer it proves is for the reader to hold.

**A topic's drawings** (R274, R280) are of two kinds. The one a topic's card shows on "Topics" is the site's data, `site/data.json` under `drawings`, by the topic's id: a small drawing of the site's own, given by its name and drawn in markup (`web/src/site/TopicThumbs.tsx`), its numbers written as the page's language writes them. A topic's page draws drawings of its own, from a module of the topic's own under `web/src/site/art/`, by the topic's slug, and a page drawn with no module stops the build: its first screen's, the cards of the ground it stands on, the key idea's table, the key under the steps of the move, each worked example's (under its task, beside its steps and beside its note) and its traps'. Their words are the page's, read under each place's key, and a drawing that is a picture of one of the kinds (4.4) is drawn as a card draws one, by the card's own functions, in the page's language. Every drawing is hidden from a screen reader, since the page's words say what it shows, and has no caption. The build refuses a drawing of a topic the catalog does not have, a card with no drawing or with a name the site does not draw, and a member that no topic's drawings, card or example has, a first screen's or an example's drawing among them. A topic's page loads the card's stylesheet only where its own drawings draw a picture as a card draws one, which its module says, and a test of the built site holds the stylesheet to exactly the pages that draw a card or a picture.

**The page of the techniques** (R181) is drawn by `web/src/site/TechniquesPage.tsx` from its words and from the site's data, `site/data.json` under `techniques`: the groups in order, each with its techniques — a technique's name, which is its anchor on the page, the topics of the catalog it leads to, and its example's level, solver and answer — and the rows of the hint, each naming the techniques it suggests. Its parts follow the draft:
- the first screen: the heading, what a technique is for and how many there are, and the advice to try a problem before its solution, beside every technique by its group, numbered in the page's order, each a link to its card;
- where to begin any problem: Pólya's four steps;
- each group — to see a problem, to find the answer, to prove — with its techniques, each a card: its number and name, a second name where it has one, what it is and when it helps, and a problem set at its example's level; then its solution — the steps, the answer and a drawing, which shows the answer too — folded under "Show the solution", a `<details>` that opens with no script; then the topics it leads to, each linking to its page once published and saying "soon" until then;
- which technique to try: what a problem says, beside the techniques it suggests;
- "Add to Claude", which leads to the home page's section on connecting, and the way to every topic.

A drawing is laid out by the page from its problem's numbers in markup and classes, with no image and no script, and fits a phone's width without scrolling (`web/src/site/TechniqueDrawings.tsx`); it is hidden from a screen reader, since the steps say the same in words, and its caption is not. That it shows the numbers its problem states and its solver proves is the reader's to hold, as the words' agreement with a solver is (R172). The number of techniques the first screen states is a plural wording of the site's dictionaries, filled in from the data; the numbers "01" on are the data's order. The build refuses a technique whose name is no anchor or another's, one that leads to a topic the catalog lacks, an example at a level none of its topics is taught at — any topic, for a technique of none — a row of the hint naming a technique the page lacks, and words with another number of rows than the data. Every example is proved as a topic's is, its solver kept in `site/solvers/techniques/<name>.star` and run by `site/examples_test.go`.

**The page of the coach** (R198) shows a product of the author's own that is still in the making: a tablet app in which a companion leads a child through a problem in Pólya's four steps, which will call this service for its tasks. It is drawn by `web/src/site/CoachPage.tsx` from its words and from the site's data, and its parts follow its draft:
- the first screen: a badge that says the coach is in development, the heading and what the coach does, a button to the prototype and one to the techniques, as many as the site's data has, in the plural wording of the site's dictionaries; beside them, a screen of the prototype, the child choosing a companion, on paper of the prototype's own colour, which fades out at its foot, leads to the prototype as the first button does and has a line under it, and which drops under the words where the two do not fit side by side (R249);
- three cards: Pólya's four steps, a canvas like a notebook, a companion of the child's own;
- `#prototype`: the coach's prototype in a frame on paper of its own colour, 1320 px wide, loaded once a reader comes near it, with a line under it and a link that opens it alone in a tab of its own;
- what the prototype is, and what it only pretends to do yet: recognising handwriting and drawings, the companion's voice, problems from a photo.

The prototype is the design tool's export, `site/assets/coach-prototype.html`, shipped byte for byte at `/assets/coach-prototype.html` with nothing taken out but a `preconnect` to Google Fonts and one of the designer's notes, unused, that named a contest: a page that unpacks itself in the browser from data it carries — copies of React and ReactDOM, the tool's runtime, the companions' pictures and the Noto fonts, whose licence travels beside it as `/assets/noto-license.txt`. Its plan and its price stay as the author drew them. It speaks English alone in every language of the page and is never translated. The frame keeps the tablet's proportions, 1272 by 932, and 2 px more, so a narrow screen shows no empty band under it. The build's test holds the prototype to the site's origin, since the checker reads markup alone and much of the prototype is compressed: it unpacks every text the prototype carries, and every absolute address in them is a namespace of XML, the page a message of React's errors points to, the Babel its runtime fetches only for a component imported by address, or the key of a copy it carries; every such copy is in it, and it imports no component, by address or by name. The test also holds the prototype to the hash it had when it was last read through, so that no new export ships unread. `robots.txt` keeps crawlers from reading the prototype, which read alone would make a search result with no word of what it is, though its address may still be listed from the links to it. The screen beside the first screen's words is the author's own picture of the prototype, taken byte for byte from the second draft: `site/assets/coach-screen.png`, 909 by 540 px, at `/assets/coach-screen.png`. The page states its size and loads it with the page, as the first screen's own, and it shows the prototype in English in every language of the page, as the frame does.

**The page "About"** (R183, R200) is drawn by `web/src/site/AboutPage.tsx` from its words: the first screen — "MathTrail is made by one family" and the four of them in a sentence — beside a photograph of the four together; a card for each of them, in the order `web/src/site/brand.ts` lists them, with their portrait, the role they play, who they are in the family and what they do, under a heading a screen reader alone hears; and "Found a mistake, or have an idea?", which writes the contact address out, links the issues of the code, and offers the address and the code as buttons. The family's words go by the names their photographs do — `dad`, `mum`, `older-son`, `younger-son` — and the children by their place in the family, never by name. The photographs are WebP in `site/assets/photos/`, the four together at 900 by 1200 pixels and each portrait at 960 by 1200, made by `just site-photos` from originals kept outside the repository: cropped with their top kept, scaled, and written with the picture alone — no EXIF, no XMP, no colour profile — which a test of the kept files holds. Each is an `<img>` with its size and an `alt` in the page's language that says what it shows, with no name; the portraits load as a reader nears them. `robots.txt` closes `/assets/photos/` to crawlers, so the page is found by a search and its photographs are not found apart from it. They are the family's own, which the MIT License does not cover: `docs/self-hosting.md` says so to whoever runs a copy.

**The page "Research"** (R204, R250, `docs/research-page.md`) is drawn by `web/src/site/ResearchPage.tsx` from its words and from the numbers of one file, `research.json`, made before the build by `just research-data` and never committed. A release's file is made by `research.yml` from the release's commit, after paper A's experiments were held byte for byte to the results kept in git and paper A was built, and is handed to the site's build with the paper's named PDF, whatever placeholder it still prints (R260); a pull request's site is built from the same run and published by no one (R234, R248). The PDF and its facts are never committed: `just site-paper` makes them for a site built on a laptop. Its live numbers come from a snapshot that is committed, `site/research/live.json`. A scheduled query keeps it every day from the public views of the counts kept for years, in a table of its own, `impact_site.live`. From the 2nd to the 5th of each month the workflow `live.yml` reads it as an identity that may read that table alone, holds it to the views' rule with the bench, and, when it differs from the one `main` holds, has a GitHub App of the repository's own open a pull request with it, which merges itself once the required checks pass (R226). `just site-live` reads it the same way by hand. The build reads it from `--research`, `site/research/research.json` by default, and names the recipe when it is not there; its reader, `web/src/site/research.ts`, refuses a file whose marks do not follow from its numbers, whose counts of the product disagree with the catalog the site reads, whose constants contradict what the page says of them, or whose live numbers show a cell their own rule hides. Its parts and its look follow the author's second draft (R250):
- the first screen: the paper's title up to its colon, in two lines, which a test holds to the paper's own in each language; how many checks a task passes; the PDF with its page count, once there is a clean one, and the code; beside them a drawing of a task's way from the chat's model through a cell for each check to the child, its worked task the fence of thesis 02's options; and under a rule the reference tasks, checks, topics and traps counted, the grades and the licence;
- four theses, each a row between rules: the chat's model writes; the solver runs twice, its second run drawn from the solver's own relabelling; the answer is sealed, and the chat itself, its model and the host's record of the tools called, is what the seal does not reach (О-27); a task a little harder but within reach, the student model's formula over an axis of the guess and the corridor;
- `#student-model`: a line of the run the numbers come from and a sentence on the paper's commit; then a board, a row for each measure, its name, unit and mark beside a bar of the service's number with its 95 % interval, the goal's line and the oracle's ceiling, each labelled, and the number; and a legend of the marks the rows hold;
- `#live`: the live numbers of the latest month counted whole, from the public views of the counts kept for years (12.4, R221): the answers against the chance promised, a point for each range of it; how far they came out from it, by the range of the child's answers, a bar from nothing with a whisker of its standard error; and the share right on the rule's tasks, large and as a point over the corridor; side by side, each drawing hidden from a screen reader and its table from the eye. Before a month is counted whole, or when its children were too few, the three columns stand empty under a badge that says so, and every state names the rule a range is shown by;
- `#sources`: ideas rather than texts; the authors, each with the year they died, and their books by title, from `site/data.json` under `research`; and the reference tasks in all and by level;
- `#paper`: what the paper holds, and its PDF once there is one.

No number on it is typed by hand: a number from data is a `<data value>` element, one the text chooses is marked as given, a commit is written in `<code>` and a date in `<time>`, the words hold no digit, and a test scans the page drawn in every language for any other. The PDF is served at `/assets/paper-a.en.pdf` and linked from every language until there is one in Russian; the build copies it from beside the data and refuses it when its size or hash is not the one the data gives.

**The page "Service"** (R251) is drawn by `web/src/site/ServicePage.tsx` and the components beside it from its words and from its own module, `web/src/site/service.ts`, which holds what no language changes: the map's 28 parts in their columns and groups and the 41 links between them, the service's tools by name, the actors and steps of each scenario, the states of the task on the card and its other ways, the places its state lives, the blocks of the profile's file with their sizes, colours and writers, a token's parts and purposes, and the lifetimes in seconds. A part's words go by its id, a link's by its two parts and a scenario's steps by their order, so a step the data has and the words lack, or the words have and the data lacks, stops the build. Its parts follow its draft:
- the first screen: the heading, what the service is, five chips, and the service in three tiers — the chat, the service with its core of pure computation, and the parent's Drive — with the protocol between each two;
- `#map`: the map of the parts; choosing one fills it, rings the parts it talks to and fades the rest, and the panel under the map, which stays in sight as the map scrolls, says what the part does and what passes between it and each of the others, every part named there a choice too;
- `#flow`: a lesson call by call — a task, an answer and the parent's sign-in, a switch between them — and the task on the card as a machine of states;
- `#state`: where the state lives that a server with state would keep, and what v1 has none of on purpose;
- `#profile`: the profile's file — its size as a file usually has it or near its caps, a switch between them, the bar of its blocks against the 64 KB it was meant to keep within, and its blocks one by one with the tools that write them;
- `#seal`: a token taken apart, and the lifetimes of the sign-in's pieces on the logarithm of the time.

Its choices are radio buttons, and the rules that show what is chosen are written into its head from the map's links and the scenarios when the site is built (R170); every state is in the page. The numbers it states are words and data of the page, checked against the code by hand: nothing holds them to it, so a change to the service that moves one moves the page with it.

**The frame** (R167). The header holds the product's logo and its name; the logo, `site/assets/favicon.svg`, is every page's icon too, at an address that never changes, since a chat's list of connectors shows the icon of the connector's domain (R191); a menu of the pages that exist — "Coach" (R198), with a small yellow tag, "Beta", that says it is still in the making (R258), "Why", "Topics", "Techniques", "Research" (R204) and "Service" (R251), the two held together as the technical pages, and "About" last — which names none of the home page's sections, since the home page leads to them itself (R202); the languages, as links; and one button, "Add to Claude", which leads to the home page's section on connecting and is left out below 720 px, where the home page, a press of the logo away, leads there from its first screen (R188, R202). Every button on the site that asks to add MathTrail leads to that section. The menu is a list written in the site's code (`web/src/site/frame.ts`), each entry a page, or for the header's button a section of one, under a word of the site's dictionaries; a page joins it with the task that publishes it, and the build refuses an entry whose page the site does not have. The page being read is marked in it, and so is the page it lies under, as the page of the topics is for a topic's own, which a screen reader is told is the menu's current entry rather than the page (R258). Entries may be held together in a group, under a word a screen reader says: the technical pages stand in a capsule of the draft's greys, the page being read dark in it, and in the folded list the capsule is a block, its pages one under the other (R251); each of them says on its first screen, in a badge of the capsule's greys, that it is written for technical readers (R258). Below 1230 px, some sixty pixels above the width at which its seven entries, the coach's with its tag, stop fitting in a row in Russian, as `just site-fold` measures it in Chromium and WebKit (R198, R209, R251, R258), the menu folds behind a round button with three lines, a `<details>` disclosure that a browser opens with no script, its list dropping under the header; with no entry there is neither menu nor button. The footer holds the pages the same file lists — "Research", "About", the privacy policy and the terms, each under its own title — then the code on GitHub and the contact address, written out as a `mailto:` link, the very address the privacy policy names. It has no line about the licence or the name: the repository's `LICENSE` carries the one and the terms the other (R142, О-4), and none about which language's text prevails, which the policy and the terms say themselves (R177).

**The look.** A white page, always light — every page carries `data-theme="light"` and `color-scheme: light` — with near-black headings, grey text and blue for whatever can be followed or pressed, buttons shaped as pills, a header that stays on top of the page as it scrolls, on a blue-grey bar, and a footer that is a narrow blue-grey band in grey type (R258), the draft's palette named in the site's stylesheet under `--s-*` (R142). The text is set in Onest from the site's own domain, through its variable `--s-font`, with the system stack behind it (R157): the stylesheet declares the latin and the cyrillic subsets itself, with the ranges the font's package gives them, and the build takes the files from the package. Spaces, radii and widths come from the tokens, and the layout is written in logical properties, so that a language written right to left needs no stylesheet of its own (8.8).

**A shared link** shows a picture of the site: every page names its language's, `/assets/og-<locale>.png`, 1200 by 630 pixels, and says in a word of the site's dictionaries what it shows (`og:image`, `og:image:alt`, `twitter:card`). It is the home page of that language as the site builds it — its mark, its heading, its chips and its promise, beside the card of a task — laid out as a picture and photographed by `just site-og`, kept in `site/assets/` and looked at before it is kept. A language with no picture yet still builds, and the site's checker refuses to publish its pages: the picture a page names is a file the site serves (R188).

**The words** of every page but the two documents are YAML, a file per page and language, held to the English file by key, slot and order (R159). The page's component reads them through the build, which notes every key it reads: a key the file lacks, or a text the page never shows, stops the build. Each file gives the page's title and description, which hold no slot and no markup: they are shown as they are written.
