No task text, no option, no answer letter — neither the child's nor the correct one; `correct` is a boolean and `trap` is a catalog id. No pseudonym, no notes, no interests. No token, code, verifier, cookie or key — only a key id. No Google `sub`, no Drive permission ID, no email, no name. No Drive file id, and no file contents in an error. No `student_id`: a child is counted under a name that changes every month (12.1). No address a request came from, no city and no coordinates: of where a family is, a line carries a country's code and a state's of the United States, from closed lists, and nothing finer (R185).

The rule is not "redact before writing" but "never build the string": a log call that takes a profile is a log call waiting to leak, so the logger is given fields, and the fields are the ones in the table above (О-16, PRODUCT 6).

`chance` on `answer_recorded` is the one number of a line the child's rating sets. Beside `user` it follows the rating answer by answer, as far as two places of a chance tell it. The topic, the level, the difficulty and whether each answer was right, which the same line carries, let the rating be rebuilt too — but only from the child's first answer on, with the start guessed from the first tasks, and only while the log keeps every answer since; the chance tells it outright. That is the trade R153 makes for the one live check of the student model. No span and no label of a measurement carries it.

An `error` field is the one field whose words are not chosen from a list: the text of a failure of the service's own, of Google's or of a library, on a line about something that failed. Every such text was read in T60, and each names a fault, a status or a protocol's code, and nothing a caller sent. A request's own line is the exception that shows why it has to be read: the text of a failed write names the address at the other end, so that line gives its errors by their kind (R73).

## 12.4 What the numbers are for

Every metric of PRODUCT 6 is a count over these lines, and the MVP needs no metrics backend to get them (О-16): acceptance rate and the reasons for refusal from `task_submitted`; generation time from `task_accepted`; attempts per accepted task from the same; limit hits from `limit_hit`; the instructions version on every one of them, so two versions are never mixed (О-21).

This pass added four that matter operationally: `solver_run.steps`, which is what calibrates the sandbox's ceiling (6.6); `drive_stale_read`, which should be rare and means Drive is serving behind; repeated `drive_call` retries, which mean the call budget is wrong (05-storage); and `limit_hit` on the failed-generation ceiling, which means a topic is defeating the models rather than a family being greedy (R15).

`just report` adds the lines up into these numbers — the tasks asked for, handed in, refused and out of attempts, the checks that refused them, the attempts and the seconds an accepted task took and how many came with a picture, by its kind, the tasks written ahead, kept, handed out ready and let go, and why, with those a card took while a card could take one (R238, R252), how long `next_task` took to hand out a task written ahead, how long after the accepting hand-in a waiting card's question brought the task (R259), how the writes after the answer went, by the tool that made them (R255, R257), the limits reached and how the tools answered — by the version of the instructions and by the chat host, and the pictures once more by topic (R228), counting only the lines that name a kind or `none`. A line written before R269 says only whether there was a drawing, and the report counts it so, under its own version of the instructions. It reads every line the deployed service wrote to Cloud Logging, as far back as the log keeps them, with the instance that wrote each beside it, and a log of a local run the same way. A line about a task names the host only on `task_accepted`, which carries it for the counts below; the report takes the host of every line from the `tool_call` line of the same call — the one whose span the line names, or, on a line that names none, the one of the same request.

Three more numbers are the ones PRODUCT 6 and 12.5 are measured against. **A tool's time without Drive** is its time, from its start to its answer, less that of the calls to Drive made in its request before it answered, tied to it by the request's id and its trace together; the median and the 95th percentile stand beside those of the whole call, against the second PRODUCT 6 allows a tool, Drive aside. **The traces** are the share of the requests whose trace was kept, beside the share the newest build of the telemetry was built to keep, and the deliveries that failed, by whether their request's trace was kept — a kept trace lost its spans — and by whether they ran out of the 200 ms they are given. **The busiest minute** is the most a minute by the clock held of what a pace counts over a minute, each call and request in the minute it arrived: an account's tool calls, the one message of an account a line names it on, and an instance's requests at the MCP endpoint and elsewhere apart, beside all the instances' together and the requests the endpoint was sent for each tool call. What the platform counted of the same window — the processor and memory time the free tier is counted in, the requests, the instance time billed and the most instances that served in a minute, on average over it — is read from Cloud Monitoring by `just usage`, beside the free tier of a month.

**The numbers for grant applications** are counts over three events alone — `task_accepted`, `answer_recorded` and `topic_mastered` — kept for years where the rest of the log is kept a month. A deployment that keeps them (`analytics` in its Terraform variables) copies the lines of those events into a log bucket of their own, `activity`, and keeps them there 62 days; every night at half past one in UTC a scheduled query counts them again into tables of BigQuery that hold no identifier, and those tables are what is kept (R184, R192). The SQL is `infra/analytics/`.

- **What is counted.** A day: the children a task was handed to that day, over the seven days to it and over its month so far, and the tasks, the answers and the topics won. A week and a month: the children, told apart by one thing at a time — the chat host, the language, the grade, the country and the state the profile gives, the country of the sign-in and the cohort — each child under the value of its latest task of the period, so that the values of a dimension add up to the whole and a child who moved from one to another is counted once. A month: the tasks each child was handed and the days it was, in ranges; the answers by the months since the profile was made — right, after the hint, "I don't know" — and the most topics each child had mastered; each topic, the children who answered it and the times it was won; and the traps fallen for, by topic and grade, by topic over every grade and by grade over every topic; and the answers weighed against the chance promised as the report weighs them (R153), to tasks the rule chose, after the trial series and without the hint, every instructions version together: by the report's ranges of chance and over every range, the children, the answers, the right ones and the chances promised, and by the range of the child's answers the same with the sums the mean's error by child is read from (R221). A day has totals alone: one country's or one grade's children of a day are too often a single child.
- **Who is counted.** A child, by `learner` (12.1): a day and a month count each child once, and a week that spans the turn of a month counts a child twice. A child the load tool or MCP Inspector handed a task to that month — the hosts `load` and `inspector` (12.2) — is no child a family has, and nothing its name is on is counted. A line the log repeated counts once, and a line that names no child counts nothing.
- **Every night counts again** what the bucket holds. The counting begins on the day after the first line that names a child — the day the counting arrived on may have begun without it — and from then on every day the bucket holds whole is counted, 60 of its 62, a day nobody had a task on among them. While the bucket still holds every line since the counting began, every night counts every period again. Once it has begun to let lines go, a period is counted again only while the bucket holds every month a name in it was given for from the first of that month — what leaving out the load tool's children has to read: a month, the month it is; a week, the month it begins in; a day, the month of the first of its seven days — and then keeps the rows it was last counted to. A night that did not run is made up for by the next, from what the bucket holds. A night's counts land whole or not at all.
- **What is shown.** Exact numbers, in views for the owner's report. For anyone, views of the months counted every day and over: no group of fewer than ten children — the groups of fewer than ten of a dimension go into one, `(others)`, and while it holds fewer than ten the smallest of the rest goes in too, and a month of fewer than ten shows nothing; a topic or a trap, which a child is one of many of, only where ten children stand behind it; every number to the nearest five, and none of fewer than ten — of children, tasks, answers or topics won; a share or a mean only where ten children stand behind it. The answers weighed against the chance promised fall in many ranges for one child, so they are shown by range with no `(others)`: a range of chance, a range of the child's answers or a month over every range only where ten children and thirty answers stand behind it, its shares, means and errors to three places (R221). The page "Research" shows the latest month of them counted whole, from a snapshot a scheduled query keeps every day in a dataset of its own, `impact_site`; the identity that reads it for the site may read that dataset and nothing else (8.12.2, R226). `just impact` prints the main numbers of the latest months, the public ones or, given `private`, the exact ones, and the days no night counted.
- **What a row is.** A row of these tables counts a group, and a group may hold a single child: no row names one, and no group of fewer than ten is shown publicly.

`just report` adds the same three events up a day at a time, by the same rules — the children, the tasks, the answers and the topics won — so that the two can be laid side by side.

**The daily report** (R267). Where the counts are kept, a view of the owner's, `impact_private.daily_report`, sets each of the 31 days to yesterday, in UTC, beside what it cost the project as Cloud Billing's standard export has it so far: charged before credits, the credits, and the cost after them. Only the Billing console turns the export on, into a dataset the deployment makes for it, `billing`, and the view is made once the deployment's `billing_export` says the export's table is there. Data Studio mails a page of it to the owner every morning: yesterday and the month so far. The schedule is made by hand, and Data Studio keeps it in the time zone of the computer it was made on, so it is this deployment's owner that holds it at eight, Central time. Like the rest of these numbers it names no child, and only the owner reads it.

The report also holds every line to the rules of 12.3: an event of the table of 12.2, only the fields decided for that event, on the lines a child is counted from each field to the form it may hold — a word of a closed list, a code of a fixed form, a month, a whole number in its range, so that a `student_id` logged by mistake or a country in words is found —, and nothing shaped like an email address in any value or name. A line that breaks one is named by its event, its field and the rule, never by what it held: an event the table does not name is not named, since its words may be anything, and a field named otherwise than the service names its fields is withheld. The fields of each event are one table, kept with the report, which the audit of a whole lesson below holds the lesson's lines to as well.

The report also weighs answers against their chance (R153). It takes the answers to tasks the rule chose, after the trial series and without the hint — not those whose topic a person chose (3.6), which it names among the answers left out —, and gives them twice, each cell with the number of children its answers came from: by the chance their tasks were handed out at — how many, the chance promised on average and the share that came out right — and by which of the child's answers each was — the mean of right, as 1, less the chance, with its standard error counted by child, since one child's answers lean together. The mean stands above zero when the estimate falls behind a learning child. A cell of fewer than 30 answers says too few rather than a number, and the error of one child's answers says one child; a line the log repeated is weighed once. The answers left out are counted by why, and the tasks left unanswered beside them: those and the answers given with the hint are mostly ones that looked too hard, so the share right reads a little high by what they take away. How precisely the chat's model hits the difficulty it is asked for cannot be read from either: each task is answered once, and a model that misses by 0.25 or 0.5 logits flattens the curve of the chance promised against the share right by almost nothing.

**The later answers set against the earlier** (R199). The same weighed answers once more, of the children with answers in two ranges of their own: the earlier, from the first after the trial series to the 50th, and the later, from the 101st to the 200th, where the learners' bench's children stop. In each range the mean of right less the chance, over its answers, and the later less the earlier, with its standard error counted by child, a child's two ranges together. Each child is set against itself, so whatever leans the same way in both ranges falls out of the difference — the answers left out, a model that misses the difficulty it is asked for by the same each time —, and a child who stopped before its 101st answer is left out rather than read as an estimate falling behind. Neither range takes 51–100, which the floor under the overall step (2.2) splits at the 62nd answer. The rows are by version and host, then for each version over every host, `(every host)`, and last over every version and host, `(every version)` (R212), printed whenever the table has a row: a child who answered the two ranges in different hosts is set against itself in the last two, and one who answered them under different versions in the last alone. Releases change the instructions far more often than the student model, so the last row is the one the live check reads, and the lean a change of the instructions between a child's two ranges brings is named where it is read (`docs/learners.md`); each line keeps its version, and each version keeps its rows (О-21). The calls of the load tool and MCP Inspector, and every child either handed a task to, are left out of every row. The numbers are to three places, and a range of fewer than 30 answers says too few. A range of answers the lines name that crosses where the two begin or end, which the service writes only if it counts in other ranges, is named under the table rather than read in neither unseen.

**The masteries taken back** (R199). No line says that a topic was taken back, and `topics_mastered` falls as well when the child's level moves a topic's tasks above the level it was mastered at (2.5), so the report follows the answers. Each topic a child was shown as mastered — a `topic_mastered` line, read with the `answer_recorded` of the same call, child and topic — is followed through the child's later answers in that topic, in the order they were given, and taken back as the service takes it back: at the second wrong answer in a row there, one with the hint or "I don't know" among them, and a right answer ends the run. One the lines stop following first — at their end, or at the topic shown as mastered again, at a higher level — was not taken back while they followed it. The table gives, by version and host, over every host and, last, over every version (R212), the masteries shown, the children, those taken back by the 5th answer, from the 6th to the 10th and later, those not taken back, those settled by the 10th answer — taken back by then or followed to it —, and the share taken back by the 10th answer by Kaplan–Meier — a mastery followed for fewer answers counts for those it was followed through —, with its standard error counted by child, the share worked out again without each child in turn, both to three places. A mastery counts under the version of the answer that showed it and is followed whatever the version of the answers after it. A share that fewer than 30 settled masteries stand behind says too few. The ranges of answers and the following of a mastery are the report's own definitions, which the learners' bench reads its children by too. A child truly past a topic still slips twice in a row now and then, so the share is read beside the bench's (remark 69), never alone.

T60 audited the log against this section, line by line. A test runs a whole lesson through the service as it is built — the sign-in against a stand-in for Google, the profile in a stand-in for Drive — and holds every line it leaves to two rules: the line carries nothing a person, a model or a sign-in wrote, and it carries only the fields of its event in the table above. The same lines, written out, must add up in `just report` to what the lesson was, so that the report reads the fields the service writes. The audit found one line that broke the first rule, `task_skipped`, which took its topic from the file as a person may have typed it; it is now held to the catalog. The table of fields the test held lines to was the lesson's alone; T64.2 made it every event's and moved it into the report, so that the log of a deployment is held to the same rules as the lesson. T64 is where the numbers first come from real load.

## 12.5 Traces and metrics

A second layer, above the lines and not instead of them. Everything section 12.4 counts is still counted from the log, without a backend and without a query language. What a trace adds is the shape one line cannot hold: which call happened inside which request, in what order, and how long each part took.

**Where it goes.** Spans and measurements are posted as OTLP over HTTP to `https://telemetry.googleapis.com`, which is the platform's own standard endpoint; each signal's path — `/v1/traces`, `/v1/metrics` — is put under that root. The requests are signed with the credentials the platform issues the service, and they name the project they belong to in two places: a `gcp.project_id` attribute on the resource, which is what decides where the data is filed, and a quota header, which is what decides who is charged for filing it. A service that has been given no project exports nothing and says so once at startup; it does not refuse to start, because a build can be rolled before the configuration that names the project reaches it.

**Who is sending.** One resource per process: the service's own name and the build it came from, and — only when something is being exported — the region, the revision and the instance underneath, asked of the platform's metadata service. A platform that answers partly costs the spans an attribute and nothing else.

**How much is kept.** A tenth of the traces by default, the configured share, whatever the request arrived saying. The frontend in front of this service traces incoming requests and puts its decision in the request, and our spans join that trace — but the decision is not the frontend's alone to make: a client sets it too, and one that asked for every request to be traced would be choosing what this service spends on traces and how long its callers wait for deliveries. So a trace that arrives is kept at the share, read off the trace's own identifier so that it is decided alike on every instance and however many requests carry it, and the spans of our own follow their parent, so a trace is kept or dropped whole. The identifier is read whole, stirred, rather than by its right half as the standard ratio sampler reads it: the frontend fills the right half of the identifiers it makes once for a run of requests, and a sampler that read that half alone kept or dropped each run whole — 0.007 of an hour's requests where a tenth was configured (R173).

**When it is sent.** A deployed instance loses its processor once a response has been returned, so anything held in memory after that may never leave. A sampled request therefore delivers its spans before it is finished, with a deadline of 200 ms: a trace is worth less than the answer, and a collector that has stopped responding must never be what a child waits for. Measurements are delivered at most once a minute, with whichever request finds them due — whether its trace was kept or not, so that a quiet instance whose requests keep none still sends them — because each delivery costs a point for every series that changed, and a minute of requests delivered together costs one; they are delivered as deltas — what changed since the last one — rather than as running totals, and side by side with the spans, each with the whole deadline. Measurements leave at no other time but one: what was measured since the last delivery leaves when the process stops. A reading with nothing in it is not posted. The reader keeps no clock of its own, because a clock that does not know when a request last delivered falls due on the same request after a quiet minute, and the collector refuses a point of a series that comes sooner than its sampling period after the one before.

That deadline has a price, and it is paid rather than hidden: a delivery that misses it loses the spans it was carrying, because a batch that failed to send is discarded rather than held for the next attempt. The delivery most likely to miss is the first one a new instance makes, which pays for the connection before it pays for anything else. This is accepted — the alternative is a child waiting on a collector. Measured against a week of real latencies, 14 deliveries of spans ran out of it beside 115 kept traces, 6 of them in the first five seconds of an instance, and it stays (R174). The report counts the deliveries that ran out of it, and whose spans they were (12.4).

**The metrics.** `http_request`, a count of requests, and `http_request_duration`, their time; `solver_run_steps`, what one run of the sandbox spent of its budget. A probe is counted in none of them and traced in neither, the same way the log leaves a successful one out: the platform asks for one constantly, and counted, they would make "requests answered" a measure of how often it checked rather than of how much the service was used. Unlike the log, which keeps a probe that failed, the measurements leave every probe out — counting only the failures would leave the number meaning something nobody could state in a sentence. Their labels are closed dictionaries — a route template rather than a path, a method from a known list, a status code, a solver status — because a label that can take any value turns one series into thousands, and every series that changed is billed in every delivery: measurements sent this way are billed as Prometheus samples, with no free allowance (R49).

**The one alert** (R266). Where the deployment names an operator, Cloud Monitoring mails them when the service has had more requests a minute, on average over five minutes, than the deployment's `load_alert_per_minute`, 300 unless set, for a minute, and again once it is back under. It reads the platform's own count of the service's requests rather than any measurement of the service's, so it holds whatever the service exports, and no alert names one of the metrics above. Google starts charging for alerting no sooner than 1 September 2027; from then the one alert is some $0.39 a month. The spend alert (T20) stays the alert on the money.

**What never reaches a span.** Everything 12.3 keeps out of a log, and nothing the lines a child is counted from add — no learner, host, language, grade, cohort, country or region is an attribute or a label —, and two more that an off-the-shelf HTTP instrumentation would add by itself: the address the request came from and the client it was sent with. They are replaced before the span is recorded. So is the text of a span's status, which the instrumentation fills with whatever errors the request collected, a failed write naming both ends of the connection among them: it is written as the span ends, where no processor can reach it, so the exporter is handed every span without it, and the code of the status alone says whether the request failed. The answer is the sharpest case: a run of the sandbox reports its status and what it spent, never the options it arrived at.

**A tool call.** Every call is a span of its own, `tools/call <tool>`, of the server kind, opened as a child of the request's span. One request is then one tree: the request, the call inside it, and whatever the tool does inside the call — a run of the sandbox, a call to Drive. The OpenTelemetry conventions for MCP suggest a different parent: the trace context a client may send inside the call's `_meta`. That parent is not taken, for two reasons. The tree would break, and a client that asked for every call to be traced would be deciding what this service keeps, which the sampling above leaves to the service (R83).

**A task's review.** Inside `submit_task` the review is two spans under the call, one for each half of 5.1: `examine_task`, which reads the task and runs its solver, with both runs of the sandbox, `solver_run`, under it; and `judge_task`, the checks, which says how the review ended — `mathtrail.review.outcome`, `accepted` or `rejected`, and `mathtrail.review.primary`, the code the attempt is counted by. The spans are opened by the transport around the calls it makes, so the checks themselves stay free of anything that watches them. Neither carries a word of the task: not its wording, not an option, not the answer.

**A call to Drive.** Every call the store makes to Drive is a span of its own, `drive <op>` — `list`, `get`, `download`, `create` or `update`, and `revisions`, `revision`, `keep` or `delete` for a file's history — of the client kind, inside the span of the tool that asked for it, so a write shows as the read, the read again and the upload it is. A call made again after a pause is the same span, and `mathtrail.drive.retries` says how many times. It carries how the call ended as `mathtrail.drive.outcome`, in the words of its `drive_call` line, and is marked failed only when Drive or the service failed: a file that is not there, access taken back, a full Drive, a file too large or a revision not kept are answers about the parent's Drive, and a call its caller gave up on is nobody's failure. A call not made because the Google token would have ended first is the service's own failure (R118). The file's ID, its name and what it holds never reach it, nor the token; the line names the span (R116).

**An answer.** Inside `submit_answer` the answer is judged and recorded under a span of its own, `record_answer`, opened by the transport around the one call it makes to the profile. It says how the answer went in the words the line about it uses and in no others: `mathtrail.answer.correct`, and for a wrong letter `mathtrail.answer.trap`, the trap by its catalog id or `other`. Neither letter reaches it — the child's or the right one — nor the pace or the rating, and an answer only told again carries neither attribute. A seal that cannot be opened marks the span as failed. No measurement is taken of answers: the line counts them (R101).

The attributes follow those conventions where they name something: `mcp.method.name`, `gen_ai.operation.name` (`execute_tool`), `gen_ai.tool.name` and `mcp.protocol.version`. The service adds its own: the instructions version, the outcome, a refusal's status, and, as `error.type`, why a call failed or was invalid. Every value comes from a closed list. The call's duration is the span's own. Only a failure of ours marks the span as failed, with a fixed description. A refusal is an answer, and a call the protocol refused is the caller's mistake. An error is never recorded on the span as an event, since events reach the exporter as they are. The call's line names the call's span, not the request's, so that a reader who found the line opens the call.

## 12.6 Coverage of PRODUCT 6

| Requirement | Where |
|---|---|
| One executable, stateless, configured through environment variables | 11 |
| $0 within the free tier, budget alert | 10 (limits), T20 (the spend alert), 12.5 (the load alert), 12.4 (the daily report of the costs) |
| Per-user limits, per-IP before sign-in, a service-wide fuse, a comprehensible message | 10 |
| The request rate in memory, the daily counter in the profile (О-15, О-24) | 10.1, 10.4 |
| The free tiers' message budget | 7.3 (`content` for text mode), 8.3 (a button spends no turn) |
| Phones first: from 320 px, finger-sized buttons, a picture that fits | 8.4, 8.8, 4.4 |
| Tools answer in under a second, Drive aside | 9.2 (timeouts), 6.6 (the solver's ceiling), 05-storage (the call budget) |
| OAuth 2.1 with PKCE, short-lived tokens, a check on every request | 9.3 |
| The OAuth state and the Google tokens sealed, the key in Secret Manager (О-7) | 9.3, 11.2 |
| No secrets in the repository | 11.2 |
| Minimal data, no personal data in the logs | 12.3 |
| A privacy policy and terms published | Outside the code: T19 |
| Aggregates in the logs: outcome, reason, time, attempts, limit hits, instructions version (О-16, О-21) | 12.2, 12.4 (`just report`) |
